Reference ID: 5864804

Dosing Regimen 75 mg orally once daily, with or without food
Applicant Proposed Camizestrant (ETCAMAH) is indicated in combination with a
Indication(s)/Population(s) CDK4/6 inhibitor (palbociclib, ribociclib or abemaciclib) for
the treatment of adult patients with hormone receptor (HR)
positive, human epidermal growth factor receptor 2 (HER2)
negative, locally advanced or metastatic breast cancer upon
emergence of ESR1 mutation during first-line endocrine-based
therapy, based on an FDA approved test
Recommendation on Accelerated Approval
Regulatory Action
Recommended ETCAMAH in combination with a CDK4/6 inhibitor
Indication(s)/Population(s) (abemaciclib, palbociclib, or ribociclib) is indicated for the
(if applicable) treatment of adult patients with hormone receptor (HR)
positive, human epidermal growth factor receptor 2 (HER2)
negative, locally advanced or metastatic breast cancer upon
detection of ESR1 mutation during aromatase inhibitor and
CDK4/6 inhibitor therapy, based on an FDA authorized test.
This indication is approved under accelerated approval based
on progression free survival as measured from detection of
ESR1 mutation. Continued approval for this indication may be
contingent upon verification and description of clinical benefit
in a confirmatory trial(s).

In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,

The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA

Reference ID: 5864804

1 Executive Summary
Product Introduction
Camizestrant is an oral estrogen receptor antagonist. It does not have approval for any indication
in the United States.
The Applicant proposed the following indication for approval in NDA 220359:
Camizestrant (ETCAMAH) is indicated in combination with a CDK4/6 inhibitor (palbociclib,
ribociclib or abemaciclib) for the treatment of adult patients with hormone receptor (HR)
positive, human epidermal growth factor receptor 2 (HER2) negative, locally advanced or
metastatic breast cancer upon emergence of ESR1 mutation during first line endocrine based
therapy, based on an FDA approved test.
The FDA review team recommends an accelerated approval for the following indication:
ETCAMAH in combination with a CDK4/6 inhibitor (abemaciclib, palbociclib, or ribociclib) is
indicated for the treatment of adult patients with hormone receptor (HR) positive, human
epidermal growth factor receptor 2 (HER2) negative, locally advanced or metastatic breast
cancer upon detection of ESR1 mutation during aromatase inhibitor and CDK4/6 inhibitor
therapy, based on an FDA authorized test.
This indication is approved under accelerated approval based on progression free survival as
measured from detection of ESR1 mutation. Continued approval for this indication may be
contingent upon verification and description of clinical benefit in a confirmatory trial(s).

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
The review team recommends an accelerated approval for camizestrant in accordance with 21
CFR Part 314 Subpart H. This recommendation is based on efficacy and safety data from the
SERENA-6 trial with confirmatory evidence from the SERENA-2 trial.
27
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

SERENA-6 is an ongoing, randomized (1:1), double-blind, placebo-controlled, two-part clinical
trial in patients with estrogen receptor-positive (ER+), HER2- locally advanced or metastatic
breast cancer (MBC) who had been treated with an aromatase inhibitor (AI) and CDK4/6
inhibitor (CDK4/6i) for at least six months. In the first part, testing of 3,256 patients for ESR1
mutation (ESR1m) in circulating tumor DNA (ctDNA) occurred every two to three months.
Patients with an ESR1m detected and no evidence of disease progression were screened for the
second part of the trial. In the second part, 315 eligible patients were randomly assigned (1:1) to
either switch endocrine therapy (ET) to camizestrant with continued CDK4/6i or to continue AI
in combination with a CDK4/6i. The primary endpoint was progression-free survival (PFS),
defined as time from randomization at detection of ESR1m until radiographic disease progression
or death. Progression-free survival 2 (PFS2) and overall survival (OS) were key secondary
endpoints.
SERENA-6 met its PFS endpoint with an estimated median PFS of 16 months (95% CI: 12.7,
18.2) on the camizestrant and CDK4/6i arm and 9.2 months (95% CI: 7.2, 9.5) on the AI and
CDK4/6i arm (HR 0.44, 95% CI: 0.31, 0.60; p<0.00001) at data cutoff 1 (DCO1). The trial also
met its PFS2 endpoint at DCO3. Current OS data at DCO3 are immature representing 58%
information fraction and the corresponding interim OS results were not statistically significant
with a HR of 0.87 (95% CI: 0.57, 1.30). A final analysis of OS at DCO4 is still planned.
Although the trial met its PFS and PFS2 endpoints, interpretation of the efficacy results was
challenging for several reasons. First, the treatment strategy implemented in SERENA-6, where
patients start a new treatment regimen at the earlier timepoint of ESR1m detection rather than at
radiographic progression, is new. Typically, for the treatment of HR+HER2- MBC, patients
receive one treatment regimen from metastatic diagnosis until evidence of radiographic disease
progression and then start a new treatment regimen, with the goal of maximizing the benefit from
each line of therapy to extend life. No prior trial or other body of evidence has established that
the experimental approach of switching treatment at ESR1m detection rather than at radiographic
progression improves long-term outcomes for patients. The SERENA-6 trial was not designed to
answer this question as no comparison of changing therapy at the earlier timepoint of ESR1m
detection to changing treatment at the standard timepoint of radiographic progression was built
into the trial design.

28
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

Second, the review team had uncertainty regarding the clinical meaningfulness of the PFS result.
Typically, trials in HR+HER2- MBC randomize patients at times when a new treatment is clearly
necessary (e.g., at metastatic diagnosis or after radiographic disease progression on current
therapy). In SERENA-6, patients were randomized at detection of ESR1m, which is not typically
associated with signs or symptoms of disease progression. It is not clear that a switch to a new
treatment regimen is needed at this earlier time, particularly as patients on the control arm were
able to remain on their current therapy for a median of approximately 9 months following
randomization. The clinical meaningfulness of a PFS improvement measured from detection of a
resistance mutation and the magnitude of improvement which would translate into improved
long-term outcomes are uncertain.
In addition, despite the PFS2 endpoint being met, FDA does not typically use this efficacy
endpoint for regulatory decision making as it does not isolate the treatment effect of the
experimental drug. Other specific concerns with PFS2 in SERENA-6 included lack of
standardization in choice and timing of subsequent treatments, and variability in assessments
where investigators could determine a PFS2 event based on RECIST v1.1 radiographic
progression, non-RECIST radiographic progression, or clinical progression. PFS2 also did not
address whether the experimental strategy of receiving the new treatment at ESR1m detection
was beneficial compared to receiving it at radiographic disease progression. PFS2 compared
receiving two new regimens on the experimental arm to continuing one previous regimen and
receiving one new regimen on the control arm, without any assessment of whether it would be
better to receive the first new therapy at ESR1m detection or at radiographic disease progression.
Current OS data are also not supportive of clinical benefit. OS is immature at a 58% information
fraction and was not statistically significant at interim analysis.
Regarding toxicity, the safety profile of camizestrant in combination with a CDK4/6i was
generally as expected for an ET and CDK4/6i, with the additional notable risk of cardiotoxicity.
Camizestrant causes reduction in heart rate (e.g., bradycardia) and is associated with QTc
interval prolongation when used in combination with a CDK 4/6 inhibitor. However, there is
insufficient information to characterize the effect size of camizestrant on the QTc interval. When
camizestrant is combined with drugs that can prolong the QTc interval (such as ribociclib which
also increases the exposure of camizestrant through CYP3A inhibition), such patients have
increased risk of life threatening arrhythmia or sudden death. One patient in the camizestrant
development program who received camizestrant with ribociclib experienced bradycardia, QT
prolongation (QTc interval >500 msec) and, ultimately, Torsades de Pointes (TdP). Further
29
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

characterization of the effect of camizestrant on the QTc interval is needed to better understand
the cardiac risk, and this will be addressed as part of the post-marketing requirements.
Given the uncertainty in interpretation of the SERENA-6 results, the FDA review team discussed
this trial at an Oncology Drugs Advisory Committee (ODAC) meeting. The majority of members
thought that clinical benefit had not been demonstrated for camizestrant because it was uncertain
whether patients should switch treatment at detection of an ESR1m or wait until radiographic
progression for better long-term outcomes, and the SERENA-6 trial did not answer this question.
Because the treatment approach in SERENA-6 represents a paradigm change for treatment of
HR+HER2- MBC, greater certainty that this strategy is beneficial is needed. The committee
generally felt that the safety profile of camizestrant was manageable.
Overall, the SERENA-6 trial met its primary PFS endpoint measured from detection of ESR1
mutation with a HR of 0.44 (95% CI: 0.31, 0.60; p<0.00001) but there is uncertainty as to
whether this result constitutes clinical benefit and further verification is needed. Although PFS
results have supported regular approvals for HR+HER2- MBC, the PFS endpoint in SERENA-6
is different from PFS used in other trials because measurement of PFS starts from randomization
at detection of ESR1m and interpreting the magnitude of effect is challenging. Compounding this
uncertainty, SERENA-6 employed an experimental treatment strategy of changing treatment at
detection of ESR1m but was not designed to show whether this strategy was superior to the
standard approach of changing treatment at radiographic progression. Although PFS2 and OS
results were directionally consistent with PFS, FDA did not consider the PFS2 endpoint
interpretable, and OS data are immature. Taken together, the PFS improvement observed in a
biomarker-selected population with an unmet medical need provides a sufficient basis for
accelerated approval, with PFS measured from ESR1m detection considered reasonably likely to
predict clinical benefit pending verification of benefit.
For verification of clinical benefit, the Applicant has agreed to conduct a new trial to compare the
early treatment-switching strategy to the standard treatment-switching strategy. This new trial
will enroll patients with HR+HER2- MBC who are receiving first-line treatment with an AI and
CDK4/6i for at least 6 months. Patients will be screened for ESR1m and patients with an ESR1m
detected will be randomized to either switch treatment to camizestrant and CDK4/6i or continue
with AI and CDK4/6i and switch to camizestrant with or without CDK4/6i at radiographic
progression. The primary endpoint will be time to strategy failure (TSF) defined as time from
randomization to either progressive disease by RECIST v1.1 while receiving camizestrant with
30
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

or without CDK4/6i, or death, whichever occurs first. This trial will not isolate the effect of
camizestrant because it will be administered on both arms, but it will allow for assessment of the
treatment strategy.
Alternately, even though FDA does not typically require a statistically significant OS result for
approvals in HR+HER2- MBC, final OS results from SERENA-6 showing a statistically
significant improvement could overcome some of the uncertainties with the current data. While
OS is also measured from randomization at detection of ESR1m and would not allow
determination of whether an early switch in treatment at detection of an ESR1m is better than the
standard switch at radiographic disease progression, a statistically significant improvement in OS
measured from any starting point may still be clinically meaningful.
The review team recommends accelerated approval for the following indication:
ETCAMAH in combination with a CDK4/6 inhibitor (abemaciclib, palbociclib, or ribociclib) is
indicated for the treatment of adult patients with hormone receptor (HR) positive, human
epidermal growth factor receptor 2 (HER2) negative, locally advanced or metastatic breast
cancer upon detection of ESR1 mutation during aromatase inhibitor and CDK4/6 inhibitor
therapy, based on an FDA authorized test.
This indication is approved under accelerated approval based on progression free survival as
measured from detection of ESR1 mutation. Continued approval for this indication may be
contingent upon verification and description of clinical benefit in a confirmatory trial(s).

Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness (SEE) was established with one adequate and wellcontrolled clinical investigation and confirmatory evidence.
Interpretation of SEE is based on the FDA Draft Guidance for Industry, “Demonstrating
Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness for Human Drug and Biological Products” (June 2026).
HR+HER2- MBC is incurable, representing a serious and life-threatening condition, and some
degree of regulatory flexibility may be applied. SERENA-6 is a randomized, controlled trial
which met its primary PFS endpoint. However, there is uncertainty in this result because of the
PFS endpoint definition and because of the SERENA-6 treatment strategy which does not have
established clinical benefit. Verification of clinical benefit is needed, as described above.

2 Therapeutic Context
Analysis of Condition
The Applicant’s Position:
Breast cancer ranks as the second most prevalent cancer globally, accounting for 11.6% of all
cancer cases. It is the most commonly diagnosed cancer in women, comprising 23.8% of all
cancer diagnoses. Male breast cancer is a rare disease accounting for less than 1% of all breast
carcinomas diagnosed in the US each year (Hasset et al 2020). In 2022, over 2.3 million new
cases of breast cancer were identified worldwide (Bray et al 2024). It is estimated that in the US
in 2025, there will be 319,750 new cases of breast cancer diagnosed, leading to an estimated
42,680 deaths due to the disease (Siegel et al 2025).
Breast cancer is categorized into different histopathological subtypes based on expression of ER,
PgR, and HER2. Approximately 70% of patients present with HR-positive (ER-positive and/or
PgR-positive), HER2-negative disease (American Cancer Society 2024-2025), making it the
most common subtype in both females and males.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s characterization of HR+HER2- MBC.
HR+HER2- MBC is incurable, and the goal of therapy is palliative.

Analysis of Current Treatment Options
The Applicant’s Position:
Endocrine-based therapy is the mainstay treatment for hormone receptor-positive,
HER2-negative breast cancer. ET include AIs, SERMs, and SERDs (ASCO Guidelines 2024,
Gennari et al 2021, NCCN 2024).
Treatment guidelines across many regions have established ET (either an AI or a SERD) in
combination with a CDK4/6i (palbociclib, ribociclib, or abemaciclib) as the SoC in the 1L for
hormone receptor-positive, HER2-negative ABC in postmenopausal women, premenopausal
46
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

women (in combination with an LHRH agonist), and men (preferably in combination with an
LHRH agonist) (Cardoso et al 2020, Gennari et al 2021, Hasset et al 2020, NCCN 2024).
Despite the proven efﬁcacy of ET + CDK4/6i for ABC, resistance invariably develops, and most
patients succumb to their disease. Second and later line endocrine-based treatments include
endocrine monotherapy and ET + targeted therapy. None of these regimens have shown OS
improvement in clinical trials. After the exhaustion of endocrine-based therapy, patients
eventually require treatment with chemotherapy, including antibody-drug conjugates (Moy et al
2022). Disease progression and the use of chemotherapy are associated with deterioration in QoL
(Giuliano et al 2019).
A common resistance mechanism to current ET is the emergence of ESR1m, the gene encoding
ERα (Rinaldi et al 2020). These point mutations in the LBD of ERα promote an agonist
conformation allowing constitutive (estrogen-independent) activation of ERα (Toy et al 2013).
Various studies have reported that the frequency of ESR1m is low (approximately 4%) at the
initial diagnosis of hormone receptor-positive, HER2-negative ABC (Bardia et al 2020, Goetz et
al 2024, Hortobagyi et al 2018). However, approximately 40% of patients at the end of
1L treatment of AI + CDK4/6i have detectable ESR1m in ctDNA, and the prevalence of ESR1m
rises further to > 50% in patients who have been treated with AI + CDK4/6i for more than
one year (Bidard et al 2022, Bhave et al 2024, Cardoso et al 2023, Chaudhary et al 2024).
Evidence suggests that detection of ESR1m is associated with poor treatment outcomes in terms
of PFS and OS (Turner et al 2020, Turner et al 2023), mainly owing to the lack of effective
treatment options to address this driver mutation. Currently, the only approved treatment
targeting ESR1m (elacestrant) is in the 2L ABC setting for patients with disease progression
following at least one line of ET including CDK4/6i. However, the treatment effect in this
population is modest, with a median PFS of 3.8 months for elacestrant vs 1.9 months of
fulvestrant monotherapy (Elacestrant SmPC, Shah et al 2024).
Considering the limited efficacy of endocrine-based treatment post-CDK4/6i and that many
patients no longer respond to endocrine-based therapy, there is a high unmet need to identify the
patients that could benefit from therapies that address endocrine resistance to current ET in 1L,
ahead of disease progression. An early switch of the ET backbone to camizestrant in patients on
1L treatment with a CDK4/6i, upon emergence of ESR1m, has the potential to prolong benefit on
1L therapy by suppressing and delaying resistance to treatment that eventually leads to clinical
disease progression and decline in QoL. Extending the duration of benefit on 1L therapy
translates into better patient outcomes, addressing this important medical need.
The FDA’s Assessment:
47
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of the current treatment landscape for
HR+HER2- MBC. Endocrine therapy (an AI or fulvestrant) in combination with a CDK4/6i is
the preferred 1L therapy, with some combinations demonstrating an OS benefit compared to ET
alone. CDK4/6 inhibitors are also approved with fulvestrant following disease progression on
ET. The choice of therapy in later lines depends on prior therapy and may include ET alone or
ET in combination with targeted therapy (e.g., exemestane in combination with everolimus)
including some options based on specific tumor mutations, (fulvestrant with alpelisib for
PIK3CA mutation, fulvestrant with capivasertib for PIK3CA/AKT1/PTEN alterations).
As noted by the Applicant, elacestrant is an oral estrogen receptor antagonist which was
approved in the US on January 27, 2023, for patients with ESR1m ER+HER2- MBC based on
the results of the EMERALD trial which had a control arm of fulvestrant or an AI (Elacestrant
USPI). Additionally, imlunestrant was approved in the US on September 25, 2025, for patients
with ESR1m ER+HER2- MBC based on the results of the EMBER-3 trial which had a control
arm of fulvestrant or exemestane (Imlunestrant USPI). Vepdegestrant was approved on May 1,
2026, for patients with ESR1m ER+HER2- MBC based on the results of the VERITAC-2 trial
which had a control arm of fulvestrant (Vepdegestrant USPI). The ideal sequencing of therapies
for patients eligible for multiple ET-based options is currently unknown.
The results of EMBER-3 Arm C (imlunestrant with abemaciclib) have also been made public,
suggesting that combinations of oral estrogen receptor antagonists with CDK4/6i for second-line
therapy may be emerging (Jhaveri et al 2024).
There are no FDA approved therapies which are given based on detection of an ESR1m prior to
radiographic progression. Cross-trial comparisons of time-to-event endpoints, such as PFS,
between EMERALD, the monotherapy trial for elacestrant, and SERENA-6 are not interpretable,
since variability in baseline demographics and disease characteristics could lead to differences in
the treatment effect observed across trials. Any comparison to SERENA-6 is further complicated
by the new starting point for PFS at ESR1m detection rather than at radiographic progression.
Finally, in SERENA-6, patients continued CDK4/6i therapy after randomization; camizestrant
was not administered as monotherapy.

48
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

The FDA disagrees with the Applicant’s position that switching therapy ahead of disease
progression provides a clinical benefit. SERENA-6 was not designed to evaluate switching
therapy at ESR1m detection compared to the standard approach of switching at radiographic
progression. The FDA also disagrees with the premise that SERENA-6 evaluated a strategy to
extend 1L treatment, which consists of an AI and CDK4/6i. Patients enrolled to the experimental
arm in SERENA-6 started a new treatment, camizestrant, and only continued the CDK4/6i
portion of the 1L regimen.
The FDA does not consider there to be any available therapies specifically for patients who have
ESR1m detected in the absence of radiographic progression. However, it is not clear that
changing therapy at ESR1m detection, prior to radiographic progression, benefits patients. More
evidence is needed to determine if the early switch approach is beneficial compared to the
standard switch approach.

Camizestrant has not been marketed or approved for commercial use by any country.

The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position.

Type B: Pre-NDA Meeting (April 4, 2025): The FDA communicated that the NDA for
camizestrant may be submitted. However, whether the results supported approval would
be determined during the review. The FDA also requested that the Applicant provide a
summary of the second-line (2L) therapies patients received by treatment arm in the
NDA submission.
FDA meeting communications, including written responses provided at Type C and Type
B meetings, represent the Agency's preliminary views based on information available at
the time and do not constitute a commitment to approve a product or a determination that
the data will ultimately support approval. This principle was explicitly reflected in the
FDA's Type B Pre-NDA meeting response (April 4, 2025), which communicated that
while the NDA for camizestrant could be submitted, whether the results supported
approval would be determined during the review. The FDA's concern regarding the
clinical rationale for switching therapy prior to radiographic progression was first raised
at the Type C Pre-Phase 3 meeting (February 3, 2021) and has not been resolved by the
SERENA-6 data; the FDA's concerns regarding the clinical rationale for switching
therapy prior to radiographic progression have been maintained throughout the
development program and are reflected in the requirement for post-marketing verification
of clinical benefit.
Fast Track Designation (FTD; May 26, 2022): Camizestrant received FTD based on
results from SERENA-1, a phase 1 dose escalation and dose expansion trial, showing that
3 out of 13 patients with measurable ER+HER2- MBC and an ESR1m who received
camizestrant 75 mg monotherapy experienced a partial response.
Breakthrough Therapy Designation (BTD; May 23, 2025): The FDA confirms that it
granted a BTD to camizestrant based on preliminary evidence of improved PFS in a
unique patient population with a life-threatening disease in SERENA-6. A drug qualifies
for BTD if it is intended to treat a serious or life-threatening condition and preliminary
clinical evidence indicates it may demonstrate substantial improvement over available
therapies. Granting a BTD does not constitute substantial evidence of effectiveness or a
favorable benefit-risk assessment for camizestrant, or a commitment for approval. BTD is
granted based on preliminary clinical evidence that a drug may demonstrate substantial
improvement over available therapies, a standard that is distinct from the evidentiary
threshold required for approval. The preliminary evidence of improved PFS that
supported the BTD grant does not resolve the fundamental questions regarding the
clinical meaningfulness of the PFS endpoint in SERENA-6 or whether changing therapy
at ESR1m detection, prior to radiographic progression, ultimately benefits patients.

5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
Executive Summary
The FDA’s Assessment:
Camizestrant (Etcamah) is an estrogen receptor (ER) antagonist proposed for treatment in
combination with a CDK4/6 inhibitor (palbociclib, ribociclib, or abemaciclib) in adult patients
with HR-positive, HER2-negative, locally advanced or metastatic breast cancer upon detection of
ESR1 mutation during aromatase inhibitor and CDK4/6 inhibition therapy, based on an FDAauthorized test. The recommended dosage and administration of camizestrant is 75 mg orally, once
daily with or without food. The nonclinical development program for camizestrant consisted of
studies in mice, rats, and dogs to evaluate pharmacology (primary, secondary, safety), metabolism,
general toxicity, reproductive and developmental toxicity, and genotoxicity. During the
nonclinical development, camizestrant was referred to as AZD9833 and AZ14066724.
Estrogen receptors (ERs) consist of nuclear ERs, extra-nuclear ERs, and G protein-coupled
ERs. Estrogen receptor-alpha (ERα) and estrogen receptor-beta (ERβ) are located in the nucleus
and are encoded by the ESR1 and ESR2 genes, respectively. ERα is expressed in breast, uterus,
liver, and bone tissues. Mutations in the ESR1 gene contribute to ER+HER2- breast cancer
resistance to endocrine therapy.
In primary pharmacology studies, camizestrant bound to the ligand binding domain (LBD) of
wild-type ERα and ERβ with IC50 values of 2.4 nM and 11.3 nM, respectively, and to the LBD
of ERα containing clinically relevant ESR1 mutations (Y537N, Y537C, S463P, D538G, Y537S,
and E380Q) with IC50s ranging from 2 to 13.7 nM. Camizestrant induced degradation of wildtype ERα in MCF7 breast cancer cells with an IC50 of 0.2 nM through a proteasome-dependent
mechanism, and antagonized estradiol-induced progesterone receptor (PR) expression with an
IC50 of 0.3 nM. Camizestrant showed no agonist activity for ER, assessed via induction of PR
expression, in MCF7 breast cancer or Ishikawa uterine cell lines. In vitro, camizestrant inhibited
proliferation of ER+ breast cancer cell lines (IC50s ranging from 0.3 to 0.8 nM) and MCF7 cell
line with ESR1 mutations (IC50s ranging from 0.15 to 8.71 nM). In vivo, daily oral
administration of camizestrant showed anti-tumor effects that correlated with a reduction in
58
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

ERα and/or PR protein expression within the tumors in various mouse xenograft and patientderived xenograft (PDX) models bearing ESR1 wild-type or ESR1-mutated tumors at ≤ 10
mg/kg. When administered in combination with CDK4/6 inhibitors (abemaciclib, palbociclib,
or ribociclib), camizestrant showed increased anti-tumor activity compared to camizestrant
alone in ER+ advanced breast cancer PDX models.
In secondary pharmacology studies, camizestrant showed minimal activity against 195 targets
(receptors, ion channels, transporters, and enzymes) apart from ERα (IC50 = 1.8 nM) and ERβ
(IC50 = 2.6 nM), in vitro, with no targets showing activity within 100-fold of the ERα primary
target. The Applicant conducted a non-GLP study in sexually immature female Wistar Han rats
(17 – 26 days old) to evaluate the uterine weights in rats as a measurement of the potential
agonistic effects of camizestrant on ERα in uterine tissues. Once daily oral administration of
camizestrant for 3 days at 50 mg/kg resulted in significant decreased uterine weight when
compared to vehicle control. The positive control tamoxifen induced a significant increase in
uterine weight compared to vehicle control. Overall, the data suggests that camizestrant did not
have agonistic effects on ERα.
In vitro, camizestrant had non-dose dependent protein binding with an average percent free
concentration of 29.3, 15.8, and 24.7 in rat, dog, and human plasma, respectively. The blood to
plasma ratio of camizestrant at the concentration of 10 μM in human whole blood was found to
be 0.991. In quantitative whole-body autoradiography (QWBA), camizestrant showed
preferential distribution to pigmented tissues, particularly the uveal tract of the eye in pigmented
rats, with melanin binding that was reversible but persistent up to 21 days after dosing. In vitro,
camizestrant did not demonstrate phototoxicity up to 100 μg/mL. In repeat-dose toxicology
studies, exposures (Cmax and AUC0-24) generally increased with increasing dose in a more than
dose-proportional manner in rats and dogs. Animal to human exposure margins were calculated
using the geometric mean steady state clinical AUCss (1116 ng*h/mL or 2.34 µmol.h/L) at the
recommended dose.
Camizestrant had no remarkable effects on the central nervous and respiratory systems when
administered orally once daily to rats at doses up to 200 mg/kg. Camizestrant showed
cardiovascular effects in vitro using human stem cell-derived cardiomyocytes and upon oral
administration to rats and dogs. Effects in vitro included time dependent increases in action
potential, and in vivo decreased heart rate, blood pressure changes (increase in rats, decrease in
dogs), QT and QTc prolongation following single and repeated daily oral administration of
camizestrant. In female rats, cardiomyopathy was noted after 6 months of dosing at ≥25
mg/kg/day (≥11-fold the human AUC at the recommended dose). In investigational
59
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)

which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA

(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic

application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

cardiovascular studies in male Beagle dogs in the presence of camizestrant-induced
bradycardia, administration with positive chronotropic agents (atropine, glycopyrrolate) caused
increases in heart rate, but at a magnitude that was lower compared to the administration of
these agents in the presence of the vehicle control. Pharmacokinetic analysis showed that
plasma and/or brain exposure of glycopyrrolate and atropine were increased in the presence of
camizestrant, suggesting that the reduced chronotropic effects of glycopyrrolate and atropine
were not due to reduced plasma exposure. The administration of glycopyrrolate and atropine
did not affect the exposure of camizestrant. In the 9-month dog study, there was a time and
dose-dependent decrease in heart rate and QTca prolongation at all dose levels (5, 10, and 20
mg/kg/day, representing 3-fold to 49-fold the human AUC at the recommended dose), with
effects typically evident by Week 4 at higher doses and Week 13 at the lowest dose, showing
progressive increases in magnitude over time. These cardiovascular effects showed reversibility
in the 1-month dog study after a 4-week recovery period, and mechanistic studies indicated that
heart rate reduction is mediated via HCN channel inhibition at the sinoatrial node. Bradycardia
and QTc interval prolongation were noted in patients, which is noted in the Warnings and
Precautions of the label along with a boxed warning for the risk of arrhythmia with concomitant
use of QTc prolonging drugs. Information regarding increased atropine plasma and brain
exposure in combination with camizestrant was included in Section 13.2 of the label.
GLP-compliant daily oral repeat-dose toxicity studies were conducted with camizestrant in rats
(≤75 mg/kg) for up to 6-months and dogs (≤20 mg/kg) for up to 9 months. Recovery groups
were only included in the 1-month studies. Of note, 6- and 9-month rat and dog studies,
respectively, were not warranted to support the NDA for a cancer indication. Additionally, the
Applicant conducted GLP-compliant 6-week and 3-month repeat dose toxicity studies in mice
and rats, respectively, to support future carcinogenicity studies; carcinogenicity studies are not
warranted to support the advanced cancer indication for this NDA. No mortality or moribundity
was observed in the repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats. However, in the 9-month repeat-dose
toxicity study in dogs, one female at 5 mg/kg was terminated early on day 244 due to welfare
reasons related to a cystic ovarian mass causing abdominal pain, which was considered
camizestrant-related. The major target organs in the general toxicology studies in rats and dogs
were male and female reproductive organs, mammary gland, lung, heart, and liver and were
generally consistent across the short and long-term studies, with effects considered related to
the primary pharmacology and mechanism of action of camizestrant.
Hematologic changes were generally more pronounced in rats than dogs and included increases
in white blood cells (neutrophils, lymphocytes, monocytes) and decreases in reticulocytes. In
contrast, dogs showed minimal hematologic changes with slight increases in fibrinogen.

Clinical chemistry alterations were also more notable in rats, with increases in alkaline
phosphatase, GLDH, bile acids, phosphorus, and potassium, along with decreases in cholesterol
and albumin; dogs exhibited minimal changes limited to slight increases in phosphate. In rats,
pulmonary macrophage aggregation (confirmed as phospholipidosis in the 1-month study;
partially reversible) was noted at ≥10 mg/kg and hepatic bile duct epithelial vacuolation at 75
mg/kg (reversible in the 1-month study); these findings were not observed in dogs.
Camizestrant administration resulted in hyperplasia and/or hypertrophy of female mammary
glands at ≥10 mg/kg in rats and 20 mg/kg in dogs (≥ 2.2 and 47.2 times the human AUC at the
recommended dose, respectively), ovarian granulosa cells at ≥5 mg/kg in rats (≥0.5 times the
human AUC at the recommended dose), sex chord stroma at 20 mg/kg in dogs, endometrium of
uterus and cervix at 10 mg/kg in dogs (≥11.5 times the human AUC at the recommended dose),
and Leydig cells in testis at ≥5 mg/kg in dogs (≥3.5 times the human AUC at the recommended
dose). Additionally, benign ovarian granulosa cell tumors were noted at 75 mg/kg in rats and at ≥5
mg/kg in dogs (57 and ≥3.1 times the human AUC at the recommended dose, respectively). Leydig
cell adenoma in the testis was noted in a single animal at 10 mg/kg in dogs (14 times the human
AUC at the recommended dose). Mammary gland effects were reversible after a recovery period
in the 1-month rat study. During review of the labeling, the FDA edited Section 13.2 of the label
to include the reproductive organ findings. Refer to Section 11 for more details.
Camizestrant was not mutagenic in a bacterial reverse mutation (Ames) assay, clastogenic in
an in vitro mouse lymphoma micronucleus assay, or genotoxic in an in vivo rat bone marrow
micronucleus test. Carcinogenicity studies were not warranted in accordance with ICH S9
guidance.
Fertility assessments in female rats were evaluated in an oral fertility and embryofetal
development study that was conducted in two phases, a main phase and a recovery phase. In
the main phase, female rats received oral camizestrant at 0, 0.001, 0.075, or 0.75 mg/kg/day for
14 days pre-mating, during mating, and through gestation day (GD) 6, and then were necropsied
on gestation days 12-14 to assess fertility parameters. In the recovery phase, female rats
received oral camizestrant at 0 or 0.75 mg/kg/day for 21 days, followed by a 4-week drug free
period before mating, and were necropsied on GD 12-14 to evaluate reversibility of fertility
effects. Daily oral administration of camizestrant to female rats in the main phase led to altered
estrous cycles, mating behavior, and decreased number of pregnancies. Effects on fertility were
fully reversible following a 4-week treatment free period prior to mating. In general toxicology
studies, daily oral administration of camizestrant up to 6 months in female rats and 9 months in
dogs resulted in atrophy in the uterus, cervix and vagina at ≥ 10 mg/kg in rats and ≥ 5 mg/kg in

dogs (≥ 2 times the human AUC at the recommended dose). Additionally, increased ovarian cyst
at ≥ 10 mg/kg and atrophy at ≥ 25 mg/kg in rats (≥ 10 times the human AUC at the recommended
dose) were also observed. Fertility assessment in male rats was included in the 6-month general
repeat-dose toxicology study in which male rats, after 9 weeks of dosing with camizestrant at
0, 10, 25, or 75 mg/kg/day, were mated with un-dosed female rats and assessment of mating
performance, sperm parameters, and pregnancy outcomes were noted. Camizestrant
administration led to dose dependent effect on male fertility including lower spermatid/sperm
counts at ≥ 10 mg/kg, increased sperm abnormalities at ≥ 25 mg/kg (≥ 6 times human AUC at the
recommended dose), and lower sperm motility and reduced mating and pregnancy rates at 75
mg/kg (25 times human AUC at the recommended dose) when mated with un-dosed female rats.
To assess effects of camizestrant on embryo-fetal development, the Applicant conducted an
embryo-fetal development study with pre- and post-natal assessments in rats. The study was
conducted in 3 phases in parallel. Phase 1 assessed fallopian transport and embryo-fetal
development (dosing GD 2-20 at 0.1 mg/kg/day), Phase 2 examined organogenesis and embryofetal development (dosing GD 6-16 at 0.001, 0.01, 0.024/0.030, or 0.073/0.091 mg/kg/day), and
Phase 3 evaluated effects on pregnancy, parturition, and lactation with pre- and post-natal
assessments (dosing GD 6 to lactation [LD] 6 at 0.075 or 0.75 mg/kg/day). In Phase 1, daily oral
administration of camizestrant to female rats at 0.1 mg/kg from GD 2 to 20 resulted in no
pregnancy. Phase 2 data were inconclusive due to low doses and limited exposure. Given the
known effects of estrogen receptor antagonists, embryo-fetal toxicity is predicted. In Phase 3,
administration of camizestrant at ≥ 0.075 mg/kg from GD 6 to LD 6 resulted in increased length
of gestation, dystocia, decreased pup survival and pup weight. All the noted effects were observed
at maternal exposure levels below the human AUC at the recommended dose. During review of
labeling, the FDA review team edited Section 5: Warnings and Precautions to include embryofetal toxicity. Refer to Section 11 for more details.
The label advises females of reproductive potential to use contraception during treatment and
for 4 weeks after the last dose of camizestrant and males with female partners of reproductive
potential to use effective contraception during treatment and for 1 week after the last dose of
camizestrant. Based on the FDA guidance, “Oncology Pharmaceuticals: Reproductive Toxicity
Testing and Labeling Recommendations", the guidance recommends a contraception period of
5 half-lives (camizestrant half-life = 23 hours after repeated dosing) for nongenotoxic small
molecules that cause teratogenicity or embryo-fetal lethality, which would correspond to 1
week. The Applicant proposed a 4-week contraception period for females of reproductive
potential guided by the rat reproductive toxicity data, demonstrating no camizestrant-related
effects on fertility following a 4-week treatment-free period; and pharmacokinetic calculation

Referenced NDAs, BLAs, DMFs

Pharmacology
Primary Pharmacology CTD 4.2.1.1
The Applicant’s Position:
Camizestrant is a next generation oral selective estrogen receptor degrader (ngSERD) and
complete ER antagonist. Camizestrant binds to the LBD of ERα, antagonising the activity of
ERα encoded by both wild-type ESR1 and mutated ESR1, and inducing proteasome-dependent
degradation of ERα, without agonising ERα.
Nuclear hormone receptor binding (Pharmacology Report 01).
In a biochemical ligand displacement assay, camizestrant bound to wild-type ERα LBD with an
IC50 of 2.4 nM and to ERβ with an IC50 of 11.3 nM. Camizestrant bound to ERα LBD containing
a range of clinically relevant ESR1 mutations (Y537N, Y537C, S463P, D538G, Y537S and
E380Q), with IC50s ranging from 2 to 13.7 nM.
ERα degradation (Pharmacology report 02).
Camizestrant degraded ERα in MCF7 cells with an IC50 of 0.2 nM. The extent of ERα
degradation was equivalent to fulvestrant across a panel of nine ER+ cells and degradation was
shown to be dependent on proteasomal activity.
ER antagonism and agonism (Pharmacology report 02; ONC9833-012)
Camizestrant antagonized estradiol-induced PgR protein expression in MCF7 cells with an IC50
63
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

of 0.3 nM. At the whole transcriptome level, 100 nM camizestrant completely reversed all
estradiol-induced gene expression changes in three ER+ cell lines. Camizestrant did not agonize
ER (no induction of PgR expression) in the MCF7 cell line (≤300 nM) or the Ishikawa
endometrial cancer cell line (≤100 nM). In cell reporter lines expressing a broad panel of
additional nuclear hormone receptors, camizestrant showed minimal antagonist activity (<25%
inhibition) and was not an agonist for any of the other receptors.
In vitro effects on cell proliferation
Camizestrant inhibited the proliferation of four ER+ breast cancer cell lines with IC50 = 0.3 to
0.8 nM and was > 1000 less potent at inhibiting proliferation in two ER- cell lines
(Pharmacology Report 02). Camizestrant showed broad cellular activity against ESR1 mutants;
in available MCF7 models expressing 10 of the 11 most prevalent ESR1 mutations (D538G,
Y537S, E380Q, Y537N/C/D, L536H/P/R and S463P), camizestrant inhibited proliferation with
IC50s ranging from 0.15 to 8.71 nM (ONC9833-013). The combination of camizestrant (10 nM)
and a CDK4/6i (abemaciclib, palbociclib, or ribociclib) reduced the proliferation of MCF7 and
T47D cells (ONC9833-010), (ONC9833-011).
In vivo monotherapy anti-tumor studies (ONC9833-014, Pharmacology Report 04,
Pharmacology Report 03, ONC9833-006, ONC9833-007).
Oral, once daily dosing of camizestrant resulted in dose-dependent tumor growth inhibition in
the MCF7 xenograft model and HBXF079-LTED and CTC174 patient derived xenograft (PDX)
models which correlated with a reduction in ERα or PgR protein expression. Camizestrant
10 mg/kg delivered significant anti-tumour efficacy across a broad panel of ER+ breast cancer
PDX models and, in some cases, induced regression. Activity was seen in: ESR1wt and ESR1m
tumors (Y537S, D538G and E380Q), models from patients that had previously been exposed to
ET plus a CDK4/6i; and preclinical models of acquired palbociclib resistance.
In vivo combination therapy anti-tumor studies (Pharmacology Report 05, ONC9833-002,
ONC9833-008)
Camizestrant, in combination with abemaciclib, palbociclib or ribociclib, delivered significant
anti-tumor efficacy in 8 ER+ ABC PDX models, regardless of ESR1m status or previous
CDK4/6i exposure. Modelling of the tumor growth rate for each individual animal across all
PDX models and treatment groups shows that the efficacy of camizestrant across the PDX panel
is enhanced by the addition of a CDK4/6i and that the efficacy of camizestrant in combination
with a CDK4/6i is not affected by the choice of CDK4/6i.
Other In vivo studies
The major human circulating camizestrant metabolites M4 and M14 have low potency or were
inactive in ERα degradation (ONC9833-003, ONC9833-009). In a rat uterine assay in vivo,
camizestrant significantly reduced uterine weight compared to vehicle control, consistent with a

ADME/PK
The Applicant’s Position:
Absorption (4.2.2.2 and 4.2.3.2) Studies BE001974-59; 174579-D; 20115392_20146290; 506480;
509329;506464; 509313; 20377187.

Mouse: Tmax achieved 1-3 h post dose. Cmax and AUC dose-proportional, no sex difference (25-150 mg/kg).
Rat: 22% oral bioavailability (10 mg/kg). After single and repeated oral dose, tmax achieved 2-8 h post dose;
plasma exposures 2.1x (Cmax) and 2.7x (AUC) higher in females, indicating possible sex difference.
Dog: Tmax achieved ≤2 h post dose. Exposures increased more than dose-proportional (5-20 mg/kg).
Monkey: Oral bioavailability 65% (5 mg/Kg). tmax ca. 3 h post oral dose. Cmax and AUC increased
proportional to dose (5-20 mg/kg).
Human: tmax was achieved 2-4 h post dose at 75 mg. F=42.7%.
Distribution (4.2.2.3, 5.3.2.1 and 5.3.2.3 ) Studies BS001884-57; BS003400-57; BE00345-46; 186844.
Percentage unbound in plasma proteins were 21.0% (mouse), 29.3% (rat), 27.7% (rabbit), 15.8% (dog),
36.2% (minipig), 28.2% (monkey), 24.7% (human). Mean blood/plasma ratio of camizestrant was 0.991 in
human blood. The % unbound fraction of camizestrant in rat brain tissue in vitro was ~1%. High volume of
distribution (Vss) in rat (~19 L/kg), dog (~5 L/kg), and monkey (12 L/kg). In albino and pigmented rats
after oral dose, highest camizestrant concentrations in liver, adrenal, glandular tissues, and uveal tract of the
eye in pigmented rats in which melanin binding was also observed in skin. Concentrations in pigmented
skin were much higher than non-pigmented skin. The radioactivity associated with melanin persisted up to
21 days after dosing but melanin-binding appeared reversible. Low concentrations of radioactivity were
found in the brain and spinal cord indicating limited penetration of the blood brain barrier.
Metabolism (4.2.2.4, 5.3.2.2 and 5.3.2.3) Studies BE001610-83; BS001884-89; BS002558-20; BS004178-15;
YHM-033; YFN/002; TNO 2023 R10295
Hepatocytes in vitro: camizestrant undergoes multiple oxidation reactions plus N-glucuronidation. All
oxidation products (M1, M2, M5) formed in human hepatocytes also formed in mouse, rat, and dog
hepatocytes. The N-glucuronides M3 and M4 were formed only in human hepatocytes.

Toxicology
General Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
The principal target organs were male and female reproductive tissues with full or partial
recovery observed in the one-month studies after 4 weeks off dose. Effects were progressive and
considered related to the primary pharmacology. In rats there was macrophage aggregation in the
lung (confirmed as phospholipidosis) and vacuolation in the biliary epithelium of the liver.
Macrophage aggregation in the lung progressed in severity after 6 months dosing in female rats
where it was considered adverse. In the rat, cardiomyopathy was evident in females after
6 months dosing. Adverse effects on male fertility in rats were observed. Dose related, functional
cardiovascular effects were observed in the dog which showed a delayed-onset and reversed to
pre-study baseline levels in the one month study after 4 weeks off dose. In the 9-month dog
toxicity study, there was a time and dose dependent decrease in heart rate and QTca
prolongation.
6 Week Oral (Gavage) Toxicity Study in the Mouse (20377187).
Key Drug-related Adverse Findings
•
Microscopic findings - adrenal and pituitary glands (F, all doses), mammary and
mandibular salivary glands (F, all doses), liver (M all doses; F 150 mg/kg/day), kidney (F,
all doses), thymus (M, all doses), lung (both sexes ≥75 mg/kg/day), reproductive system
(M and F, all doses). NOAEL not established - M (thymus atrophy) and F (reproductive
tract).
•
GLP compliance: Yes

Test system: AZD9833 was evaluated up to cytotoxic concentrations (51 μg/mL for 3+21-hour

Study is valid: Yes. Of note, one of the replicates at the concentration of 51 μg/mL had a %
micronucleus frequency (MN%) of 0.6%, which is higher than the historical control range (0.05
to 0.5%). The Applicant attributed this higher value to high toxicity (52%) observed at this
concentration.
Study title: AZD9833: Genetic Toxicity Evaluation Using the Rat Micronucleus Test After
Two Oral Doses (GLP Study No.:8379662)
Key Study Findings: Oral doses of AZD9833 up to 400 mg/kg/day did not induce statistically
significant increases in micronucleated immature erythrocytes in the bone marrow of female rats.
GLP compliance: Yes (OECD)
Test system: Female Han Wistar rats (n=6/group) were administered two doses of AZD9833
(batch# C672-01, 98.7% purity) approximately 24 hours apart at 0, 40, 200, or 400 mg/kg; bone
marrow micronuclei were assessed.
Study is valid: Yes

The Applicant’s Position:
As indicated in the ICH S9 guidance, carcinogenicity studies are not warranted to support
marketing for therapeutics intended to treat patients with advanced cancer.

Reference ID: 5864804

Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
Camizestrant had reversible adverse effects on oestrous cycles, mating behavior, fertility and
early embryonic survival in the rat. When administered to the rat during organogenesis,
camizestrant had no adverse effect on embryofetal survival, growth or development. When
maternal dosing was extended through to parturition and into lactation the length of gestation
increased, dystocia occurred, and offspring survival and growth was impaired. When
administered to the rat during organogenesis no adverse effects on embryo-fetal survival, growth
or development. When maternal dosing was extended through to parturition and into lactation the
length of gestation increased, dystocia occurred and offspring survival and growth was impaired.
Effects were observed at plasma levels lower than those of the intended clinical dose.
Oral fertility and early embryofetal development study in the female rat/490021/4.2.3.5.1

6 Clinical Pharmacology
Executive Summary
The FDA’s Assessment:
The Applicant is seeking approval of camizestrant in combination with a CDK4/6 inhibitor
(abemaciclib, palbociclib, or ribociclib) for the treatment of adult patients with hormone receptor
(HR) positive, human epidermal growth factor receptor 2 (HER2) negative, locally advanced or
metastatic breast cancer (BC) upon emergence of estrogen receptor-1 (ESR1) mutation during
first line (1L) endocrine based therapy, based on an FDA approved test.
The clinical pharmacology review focused on the following key review issues:
1. Recommended dosage for the general population: The proposed dosage of
camizestrant is 75 mg orally (PO), once daily (QD), with or without food, in combination
with a CDK4/6 inhibitor (abemaciclib, palbociclib, or ribociclib). The proposed
recommended dosage was selected based on safety, activity, pharmacokinetic (PK), and
pharmacodynamic (PD) data from SERENA-1, SERENA-2 and SERENA-6, supportive
exposure-response (E-R) analyses for safety, and PK/PD data from SERENA-3. Refer to
8.1 for detailed information on the assessment of effectiveness.
2. Recommended dosage for patients with hepatic impairment (HI): When the indicated
CDK4/6 inhibitor in combination with camizestrant is ribociclib, no dosage adjustment of
camizestrant is recommended for patients with mild or moderate HI (Child-Pugh A or B);
however, FDA recommends reducing the dosage of camizestrant to 75 mg every other
day (QOD) administration in patients with severe HI (Child-Pugh C). When the indicated
CDK4/6 inhibitor in combination with camizestrant is abemaciclib or palbociclib, no
dosage adjustment of camizestrant is recommended for patients with mild, moderate, or
severe HI (Child-Pugh A, B, or C, respectively). Camizestrant steady-state Cmax and
AUC are estimated to increase 2- to 3-fold in patients with moderate or severe HI
receiving the combination of camizestrant and abemaciclib or palbociclib. The increased
camizestrant exposure in patients with moderate or severe HI remains within a tolerable
exposure range based on comparison of safety, tolerability, and PK data from patients
who received up to 300 mg QD camizestrant. However, for patients with severe HI
receiving the combination of camizestrant and ribociclib (400 mg), camizestrant AUC is
117
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

predicted to increase 6.2-fold, which exceeds the tolerable exposure range (see Section
6.3.2.3 for more details). Therefore, FDA recommends a dosage reduction of
camizestrant to 75 mg QOD administration in patients with severe HI receiving the
combination of camizestrant and ribociclib.
3. FDA recommends that patients with moderate and severe HI be monitored for increased
adverse reactions given the positive E-R relationships for bradycardia and visual adverse
events.
4. Dosage modifications for patients receiving concomitant strong CYP3A inhibitors:
No dosage recommendations were proposed in the labeling for concomitant
administration with strong CYP3A inhibitors given that a 2-fold increase in camizestrant
exposure following concomitant administration with strong CYP3A inhibitors (i.e.,
itraconazole, ribociclib) is within a tolerable exposure range based on comparison of
safety, tolerability, and PK data from patients who received up to 300 mg QD
camizestrant. FDA recommends monitoring for increased adverse reactions when
camizestrant is administered concomitantly with a strong CYP3A inhibitor given the
positive E-R relationships for bradycardia and visual adverse events.
5. Dosage modifications for patients receiving concomitant strong and moderate
CYP3A inducers: Avoid concomitant use of camizestrant with strong and moderate
CYP3A inducers. Based on physiologically based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) modeling,
exposure of camizestrant 150 mg QD (2 times the proposed recommended dosage) with a
moderate CYP3A inducer is similar to that of camizestrant 75 mg QD alone. Therefore,
FDA recommends increasing the dosage of camizestrant to 150 mg QD when
concomitant administration with a moderate CYP3A inducer cannot be avoided and the
indicated CDK4/6 inhibitor is abemaciclib or palbociclib. No increase in camizestrant
dosage is recommended for patients receiving camizestrant in combination with
ribociclib concomitantly with a moderate CYP3A inducer, as the exposure of
camizestrant when combined with ribociclib is already increased 2-fold.
Recommendations
The Office of Clinical Pharmacology has reviewed the information contained in NDA 220359.
This NDA is approvable from a clinical pharmacology perspective. The key review issues with
specific recommendations and/or comments are summarized in FDA Table 2.

General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
6.2.2.1.

General Dosing

Data and The Applicant’s Position:
The recommended dose of camizestrant in combination with a CDK4/6i is 75 mg once daily. The
CDK4/6i should be continued at the same dose at the time of initiation with camizestrant as
when the ESR1m was detected.
Cumulative data from non-clinical PK/PD, clinical PK, PD and efficacy support 75 mg
camizestrant QD in combination with palbociclib, ribociclib, or abemaciclib in the proposed
indication:
•
•
•
•
•
•
•

•

A dose of 75 mg camizestrant QD was well tolerated in the patient population with advanced
ER-positive, HER2-negative breast cancer, with the potential to maximize efficacy while
minimizing the overall burden of AEs on patients.
Non-clinical PK/PD indicated minimal increase in probability of achieving target ER
degradation for monotherapy doses greater than 75 mg QD; doses < 75 mg were associated
with a substantially lower probability of achieving target ER degradation.
Comparable inhibition of the biomarkers ER, PgR and Ki67 for doses from 75 to 300 mg was
observed in SERENA-3 in primary breast cancer.
No relationship between camizestrant PK and PFS over the exposure range 75 and 150 mg in
the Phase 2 randomized trial SERENA-2 was observed.
A 75 mg QD dose of camizestrant in combination with a CDK4/6i has an acceptable
benefit-to-risk profile in the intended patient population.
PopPK analysis shows no dose adjustment of camizestrant is required on the basis of age,
body weight, menopausal status, obesity, albumin, mild hepatic function, CrCL, or acid
reducing agents.
Based on cumulative assessment of observed and predicted exposures from popPK and
physiologically-based PK models, no dose adjustment of camizestrant on the basis of mild,
moderate and severe hepatic impairment (as classified using the Child-Pugh method) is
required. Camizestrant does not affect palbociclib, ribociclib, or abemaciclib PK; and
palbociclib and abemaciclib do not affect the PK of camizestrant.
Camizestrant 75 mg QD when co-administered with ribociclib QD resulted in camizestrant
exposure increase by 2.6 to 3.2-fold; this observed increase with ribociclib is consistent with
that observed in a DDI study where camizestrant 75 mg was co-administered with
itraconazole. These observations are also consistent with population based model predictions

Comprehensive Clinical Pharmacology Review
General Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetic Characteristics
Data and The Applicant’s Position:
•

•

Absorption: Following single oral administration to participants with ABC, camizestrant
was rapidly absorbed, with Cmax observed at approximately 2 to 4 h post dose. The absolute
bioavailability of camizestrant 75 mg administered QD to post-menopausal healthy
participants was estimated to be 43% (90% CI: 36.8%, 49.0%).
Distribution: The geometric mean (CV%) Vz for camizestrant was estimated to be 1430 L
(15.6%) following a single IV infusion of 100 μg [14C]-camizestrant after a single 75 mg oral
dose of camizestrant. Camizestrant was not extensively bound to human plasma proteins in
vitro (percentage unbound 24.7%). The blood to plasma ratio was 0.99.

3) A dose-dependent relationship for visual effect and bradycardia AEs was observed in
patients who received camizestrant 75 mg, 150 mg, and 300 mg QD in combination
with CDK4/6 inhibitors and exposure-dependent relationships for grade ≥1 visual
effect and bradycardia AEs were observed across the camizestrant monotherapy
dosage range of 25 – 450 mg QD (see FDA Figure 1, FDA Figure 2, and FDA Table
4). Further time-to-event analysis suggested that visual effect and bradycardia AEs
were generally managed without dosage reduction (~5% and 4 – 12%, respectively)
and did not lead to discontinuation of camizestrant + CDK4/6 inhibitor (see FDA
Table 4).
4) FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position that there are no clinically relevant DDIs
between camizestrant and abemaciclib and palbociclib based on comparison of
camizestrant PK in combination with abemaciclib and palbociclib to camizestrant
monotherapy PK from SERENA-1 (see FDA Table 5). These results are also
consistent with the PopPK analyses (See FDA Figure 3) indicating that abemaciclib
and palbociclib are not significant covariates of camizestrant PK.
5) Noncompartmental analyses (NCA) based on sparse PK sampling from SERENA-1
Parts K + L (n=30) indicated that ribociclib (strong CYP3A inhibitor) 400 mg and
600 mg QD for 21 days on/7 days off increased camizestrant steady-state Cmax and
AUC 2.4- to 2.8-fold and 2.6- to 3.2-fold, respectively (see FDA Table 5). In
contrast, PopPK modeling (n=80) estimated a 2-fold increase in both camizestrant
AUC and Cmax regardless of ribociclib dosage. The NCA-based estimates may
overestimate the magnitude of the DDI because sparse PK sampling may not
adequately capture the terminal elimination phase of camizestrant, particularly given
that ribociclib, as a strong CYP3A inhibitor, may prolong this phase. Since PopPK
modeling is better suited for analyzing sparse PK data and is informed by a larger
sample size, FDA agrees that ribociclib increases camizestrant exposure
approximately 2-fold based on the PopPK results.
The following safety analysis further supports the acceptability of camizestrant 75 mg QD in
combination with ribociclib despite the 2-fold increase in exposure. PopPK indicated that the
dosage of ribociclib (400 or 600 mg) was not a significant covariate impacting camizestrant PK;
therefore, the two ribociclib dose cohorts were pooled in the safety analysis. The incidence of
grade 3 – 4 TEAEs, SAEs, and TEAEs leading to dosage modification with camizestrant 75 mg
QD + ribociclib (400 or 600 mg QD, 21 days on/7 days off) were similar to those observed with
camizestrant 300 mg QD + palbociclib and camizestrant 300 mg QD monotherapy (4 times the

FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position that no dosage adjustments are necessary for patients
with mild, moderate, or severe HI when the indicated combination CDK4/6 inhibitor is
abemaciclib or palbociclib or for patients with mild or moderate HI when the indicated
combination CDK4/6 inhibitor is ribociclib based on the points summarized below; however,
FDA disagrees with the Applicant’s position that no dosage adjustments are necessary for
patients with severe HI when the indicated combination CDK4/6 inhibitor is ribociclib based on
the rationale summarized below:
1. PopPK Analysis: A pooled PopPK analysis indicated no statistically significant effect of
mild HI (n=209) on the PK of camizestrant compared to patients with normal hepatic
function (n=546). Insufficient data were available to draw conclusions regarding patients
with moderate HI (n=1), and no participants with severe HI were included in the PopPK
analysis.
2. Dedicated Hepatic Impairment Study (Study D8532C00002): Following a single 75 mg
dose of camizestrant administered to healthy post-menopausal women, camizestrant
AUC and Cmax were 2.3- to 2.7-fold higher in participants with moderate HI (ChildPugh B) and 3.1- to 3.8-fold higher in participants with severe HI (Child-Pugh C),
respectively, compared with participants with normal hepatic function (see FDA Table
6). No apparent differences in rates of TEAEs or SAEs were observed between
participants with normal hepatic function and those with moderate or severe HI
following administration of a single 75 mg dose of camizestrant (see FDA Table 7).
3. Steady-State Predictions in Patients with HI: To predict steady-state AUC and Cmax of
camizestrant in patients with HI, the Applicant utilized a PBPK model of virtual patients
with HI. Based on the PBPK simulations, steady-state AUC and Cmax of camizestrant
may be comparable to those observed in patients with moderate or severe HI following
administration of a single dose of camizestrant. See 19.4.3.4.5 for detailed PBPK
assessment.
4. Steady-State Predictions for Camizestrant in Combination with Ribociclib in Patients
with HI: Because camizestrant systemic exposure is increased approximately 2-fold when
co-administered with ribociclib (400 or 600 mg QD), the Applicant also simulated
camizestrant exposures in combination with ribociclib in patients with HI to predict the

5. Safety Threshold: The incidence of Grade 3–4 treatment-emergent adverse events
(TEAEs), serious adverse events (SAEs), and TEAEs leading to dosage modification
were generally comparable across the monotherapy dosage range of 75 mg to 300 mg QD
in SERENA-1 and SERENA-2, suggesting that the exposure of camizestrant at the 300
mg QD dose level (4 times the proposed recommended dose) is acceptable from a safety
perspective. Given that camizestrant exhibits nonlinear pharmacokinetics, the observed
camizestrant exposure at 300 mg QD is 5.7-fold higher than the observed exposure at 75
mg QD despite a 4-fold increase in dose. To contextualize the exposure in patients with
HI relative to a clinically evaluated exposure threshold, the FDA reviewer overlaid the
concentration-versus-time profile of camizestrant 300 mg QD from SERENA-1 and
SERENA-2 with the results from the dedicated HI study (Study D8532C00002). See
FDA Figure 4.
Taken together, at the proposed recommended dosage of 75 mg QD, the predicted steady-state
exposures of camizestrant in patients with mild, moderate, or severe HI when the indicated
combination CDK4/6 inhibitor is abemaciclib or palbociclib and in patients with mild or
moderate HI when the indicated CDK4/6 inhibitor is ribociclib are within the range of exposures
observed at the camizestrant 300 mg QD monotherapy dose level, which demonstrated an
acceptable safety profile in SERENA-1 and SERENA-2. For patients with severe HI receiving
camizestrant in combination with ribociclib (400 mg once daily x 21 days on/7 days off), FDA

8 Statistical and Clinical Evaluation
Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
SERENA-6 (D8534C00001)
Trial Design
The Applicant’s Description:
SERENA-6 is a Phase III, parallel, randomized, double-blind, 2-arm, matched placebo,
multicenter, international study assessing the efficacy and safety of switching to
camizestrant + CDK4/6i (palbociclib or ribociclib or abemaciclib) compared with continuing AI
(anastrozole or letrozole) + CDK4/6i in patients with hormone receptor-positive/HER2-negative
metastatic breast cancer that is ESR1m positive, based on ctDNA detection with the
Guardant360® CDx assay without clinical or radiographic disease progression during 1L
treatment with AI + CDK4/6i (Figure 6).

Applicant: Schematic of Study Design

Pre/peri-menopausal females or males (if medically indicated) continued to receive concurrent LHRH
agonist/antagonist during STEP 1 (ESR1m Detection Phase). During the STEP 2 (Treatment Phase), they
received concurrent monthly LHRH (goserelin or leuprorelin). Dose per local guideline or label.
Patients received the same CDK4/6i at the same dose level in STEP 1 and STEP 2.
Patients received the same AI in the STEP 1 and STEP 2.
If routine tumor assessment was performed within 28 days before STEP 2 randomization and was consistent
with RECIST 1.1 standard, no repeat baseline tumor assessment scan was needed. Therefore, it was preferable
that tumor assessment during STEP 1 was performed to RECIST 1.1 standards (CT [preferred] or MRI, with
IV contrast, of the chest, abdomen and pelvis).
If the patient was followed in the clinic every 2 or 3 months for tumor assessment scans, it was recommended
that the ctDNA blood sample collection was taken up to 14 days prior to (or on the same day as) the routine
tumour imaging assessment, and ideally in time to allow for the result to be reported to the site in time for the
patient’s tumor assessment visit.
Once the randomization cap of about 300 patients was reached and recruitment was closed, patients under
surveillance period had the option of providing a ctDNA blood sample for testing at the time of disease
progression. Patients who progressed during the surveillance phase were allowed a last ctDNA test at the time
of disease progression for detecting the mutation landscape of the tumor.

Trial Location: Patients were screened in 264 centers in 23 countries worldwide.

Reference ID: 5864804

Choice of Control Group: AI + CDK4/6i was considered to be an appropriate choice for the
control arm. This study enrolled patients who had been already treated with AI + CDK4/6i for at
least 6 months (Figure 6).
Diagnostic Criteria: The study had two steps (Figure 6). STEP 1 was the ESR1m Detection
Phase, and STEP 2 was the Treatment Phase for patients with hormone
receptor-positive/HER2-negative metastatic breast cancer that were ESR1m positive. In STEP 1,
blood samples for ctDNA testing were taken every 2 to 3 months during routine clinical visits
and screened for ESR1m. A patient with ESR1m disease was defined as having a plasma sample
which, when tested centrally with the Guardant360® CDx assay, returned a mutation in the
ESR1 gene that gave rise to at least one of the following amino acid changes: E380Q, V422del,
S463P, L536H/P/R, Y537C/D/N/S or D538G. If at any point during STEP 1 ESR1m was
detected and there was no evidence of clinical or radiographic disease progression, the patient
was then consented to move into STEP 2 of the study.
Dose Selection: The camizestrant Phase III dose (75 mg once daily) was selected based on
nonclinical pharmacodynamic-exposure relationship, and the available PK, pharmacodynamic,
efficacy (ORR, CBR24, and PFS) and safety data from SERENA-1 (see Section 6.2).
SERENA-2 and SERENA-3 studies further provided evidence that a camizestrant dose of 75 mg
maximized efficacy and minimized the overall burden of AEs on patients. The SERENA-1 study
data demonstrated an adequate benefit/risk profile for camizestrant 75 mg in combination with
palbociclib, ribociclib, or abemaciclib doses (per their respective local labels), also supporting
the selected Phase III camizestrant dose of 75 mg once daily in combination with a CDK4/6i. In
SERENA-6, The vast majority of patients were expected to have already settled on a stable dose
of their CDK4/6i and were likely to maintain the same dose level when they transitioned to the
study treatment phase of the study.
Study Treatments, Assignment to Treatment, and Blinding: In STEP 2, patients were randomized
in a 1:1 ratio to one of the following treatment arms:
•
•

Group A: camizestrant (75 mg oral, once daily) + CDK4/6i (continuing palbociclib,
ribociclib, or abemaciclib, maintained at the same dose as when ESR1m was detected) +
placebo for AI (in lieu of continuing anastrozole or letrozole).
Group B: AI (continuing anastrozole 1 mg oral, once daily or letrozole 2.5 mg oral, once
daily) + CDK4/6i (continuing palbociclib, ribociclib, or abemaciclib, maintained at the same
dose as when ESR1m was detected) + placebo for camizestrant.

The randomized, double-blind design of this study reduced the potential for bias.
