Reference ID: 5864804

Dose Modification/Dose Discontinuation:
•

•

•
•

Camizestrant: Dose interruptions were permitted, and recommended for the management of
some toxicities. In the event of any treatment interruption lasting more than 3 weeks, study
treatment was to be permanently discontinued. The dose of camizestrant/placebo could be
reduced once per patient to 50 mg once daily. If a patient receiving 50 mg once daily
required further dose reduction, the patient was to discontinue camizestrant/placebo.
CDK4/6i: The locally approved prescribing information for palbociclib, ribociclib, and
abemaciclib provided guidance on management of toxicities. Dose interruptions of
palbociclib, ribociclib, or abemaciclib were recommended for the management of known
toxicities indicated as per the product label.
AI: Dose modifications for letrozole and anastrozole were not permitted during the study.
Dose interruptions of letrozole or anastrozole were recommended for the management of
known toxicities indicated as per the product label.
LHRH: No dose modification of LHRH agonists was planned in this study. The locally
approved prescribing information for the relevant LHRH agonist provided guidance on
LHRH agonists and management of LHRH agonist-related AEs.

Administrative Structure: This study used an IDMC comprised of independent experts, which
convened to review safety data (in an unblinded manner) and make recommendations to
continue, amend, or stop the study based on safety findings. The IDMC reviewed safety data
from the SERENA-1 study for the camizestrant + abemaciclib and camizestrant + ribociclib
combination cohorts and performed benefit/risk analyses to confirm the acceptability of
abemaciclib and/or ribociclib inclusion in SERENA-6. In addition, the IDMC was requested to
review efficacy data for SERENA-6; for the interim analysis, the IDMC reviewed unblinded
interim data and informed the sponsor whether statistical significance had been met for PFS.
Procedures and Schedule: Tumor assessments using images from CT (preferred) or MRI, with IV
contrast, of the chest, abdomen, and pelvis, were collected during the screening period and every
8 weeks (± 7 days) for the first 18 months, and every 12 weeks (± 7 days) thereafter, from
randomization until first event of RECIST 1.1-defined radiological progressive disease. AEs and
SAEs were collected from the time of signature of the ICF, throughout the treatment period, at
the EoT visit, and until the follow-up period was completed. Baseline ophthalmological
examination were performed before starting treatment where feasible, but may have been
performed up until Cycle 2 Day 1, and were then performed during the study as clinically
indicated, and were performed at the EoT visit.

Dietary Restrictions/Instructions: Patients were required to refrain from consumption of large
amounts of Seville oranges, grapefruit or grapefruit juice, (and other products containing these
fruits [eg, marmalade]) during the study.
Concurrent Medications:
Prohibited medications:
•

•

•
•

•
•

Strong CYP3A4/5 inhibitors/inducers were not to be combined with camizestrant and were
not allowed in this study; sensitive CYP2B6 substrates were to be stopped at least 2 weeks
before the first dose of camizestrant; and drugs which were substrates of CYP2C9 and/or
CYP2C19 that have a narrow therapeutic index were not to be combined with camizestrant.
Ribociclib has been shown to prolong QT interval in a concentration-dependent manner.
When ribociclib was combined with camizestrant in SERENA-6, clinical monitoring and
dose modifications with reference to the approved ribociclib label were implemented,
including an exclusion criterion of mean resting QTcF interval ≥ 450 ms. Drugs known to
prolong QT and/or have a known risk of TdP were not to be combined with camizestrant.
It was advised that systemic atropine and systemic atropine-containing medicines were not
administered to patients during the study (including for surgery).
Patients requiring palliative radiotherapy or surgery for breast cancer to manage disease
worsening which occurs after randomization were considered to have progressed. If palliative
radiation or surgery was performed for breast cancer for reasons other than objective disease
progression, the patient could have continued to receive study medication and be followed
for objective disease progression, for example palliative radiotherapy was permitted for the
treatment of painful bony lesions provided that the lesions were known to be present at the
time of study entry and the investigator clearly documented that the need for palliative
radiotherapy was not indicative of disease progression.
Patients were not to receive any other anti-cancer therapy while on study.
Sex hormone containing drugs, such as hormone replacement therapy, progestational agents,
dehydroepiandrosterone, other androgens and selective ER modulators were not permitted
during the study.

Treatment Compliance: A record of the number of camizestrant/placebo tablets, anastrozole or
letrozole/placebo tablets, and palbociclib, ribociclib, or abemaciclib tablets dispensed to and
taken by each patient was maintained and reconciled with study treatment and compliance
records. Compliance was assessed by counting returned tablets/capsules during the site visits and
documented in the source documents and eCRF.
Subject Completion, Discontinuation, or Withdrawal: Randomized patients continued study
treatment until objective radiological disease progression as defined by RECIST 1.1,

unacceptable toxicity occurred, treatment discontinuation criterion was met, the patient withdrew

The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of the design of SERENA-6 and has the
following comments:
•

•
•

•

It is unknown whether changing treatment at ESR1m detection, prior to radiographic
progression, offers long-term benefit to patients. The development program for
camizestrant, including SERENA-6, also was not designed to demonstrate whether the
experimental strategy of receiving a new treatment at detection of an ESR1m is beneficial
compared to receiving it at radiographic progression.
No crossover was permitted to camizestrant and a CDK4/6i after radiographic
progression on the control arm.
Initially, only patients receiving palbociclib were allowed to enroll while the independent
data monitoring committee (IDMC) reviewed the safety data from the camizestrant
development program. Later, patients receiving abemaciclib and ribociclib were allowed
to enroll.
Although in general, strong CYP3A4/5 inhibitors were not permitted, FDA notes that
ribociclib has a dose-dependent CYP3A inhibition and qualifies as a strong CYP3A
inhibitor at 600 mg dose and a moderate CYP3A inhibitor at the 400 mg based on the
available DDI study data.

Eligibility Criteria
The Applicant’s Description:
The study enrolled adult female (pre/peri-menopausal or post-menopausal) and male patients
with a diagnosis of locoregionally recurrent or metastatic adenocarcinoma of the breast
histologically confirmed as ER-positive/HER2-negative, not amenable to resection or radiation
therapy with curative intent. During STEP 1, the ESR1m Detection Phase, if patients were
confirmed to have ESR1m positive disease, they were then deemed eligible to screen for STEP 2,
the treatment phase of the study, where they were randomized to receive study treatment. The

Study Endpoints
The Applicant’s Description:
The primary endpoint is PFS per RECIST v1.1 as assessed by investigator in the FAS, defined as
patients randomized after entering STEP 2 due to detection of ESR1m without concurrent disease
progression. PFS by BICR was conducted as a supplementary analysis. The key secondary
endpoints are PFS2 and OS in the FAS. Other secondary endpoints are the assessment of
chemotherapy-free survival, ORR, CBR24, TFST, TSST, TTD in breast and arm symptoms,
pain, and physical functioning, PK, and safety and tolerability. Exploratory endpoints include
patient reported symptoms, functioning and HRQoL.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA notes that only the PFS, PFS2, and OS endpoints were controlled for multiplicity; the
other endpoints are exploratory. The FDA has the following additional comments on the trial
endpoints:
• Regarding PFS, the starting point for randomization, after detection of an ESR1m, is
new. In 1L trials, the starting point for randomization is at diagnosis of advanced or
metastatic disease and in later line trials, the standard starting point is at radiographic
progression. Because of the new starting point in SERENA-6, the clinical
meaningfulness of a PFS improvement measured from randomization at detection of an
ESR1m is uncertain.
•

Both the PFS and PFS2 analyses result in comparisons where different numbers of new
therapies after randomization are received on each arm (i.e., one new therapy versus
continuation of a previous therapy for PFS and two new therapies versus one new
therapy for PFS2). This may have the effect of inflating the differences observed in these

endpoints without providing long-term benefit for patients who need treatment
indefinitely.
•

Despite the formal inclusion of PFS2 in the statistical hierarchy, FDA generally does not
consider PFS2 acceptable for regulatory decision-making, as communicated to the
Applicant in the February 8, 2021 Type C Guidance meeting preliminary comments.
FDA also provided feedback in preliminary responses to a Type C meeting request—
sent on November 4, 2024—that PFS2 would not be suitable as an endpoint for
regulatory decision-making. Refer to Section 3.2.

•

No assessment of PFS2 by BICR was planned.

Statistical Analysis Plan and Amendments
The Applicant’s Description:
Statistical Analysis Plan: A comprehensive SAP was finalized at the time of enrollment of the
first patient in the study (SAP Version 1.0), which detailed all analyses to be performed and
summaries to be produced, and the analysis sets upon which they were to be based. The SAP was
subsequently updated prior to the interim analysis.
Interim and Final Analyses: This study is planned to have a total of 4 DCOs: an interim analysis
for PFS (DCO1), the final analysis for PFS (DCO2), a primary analysis for PFS2 (DCO3), and a
final analysis for OS (DCO4). The assessment of efficacy and safety for this NDA was
conducted using a pre-specified analysis timepoint (DCO1).
Populations for Analysis: Analysis sets were defined as: Enrolled (all patients for whom it was
recorded on the eCRF that they met all eligibility criteria for STEP 1 [ESR1m Detection Phase]),
FAS (all patients who were randomized in the study [after entering STEP 2 due to ESR1m
positivity without concurrent progressive disease]. The FAS was used for all the efficacy
analyses. Treatment arms were compared on the basis of randomized study treatment, regardless
of the study treatment actually received. Patients who were randomized but did not subsequently
receive study treatment were included in the analysis in the treatment arm to which they were
randomized. The analysis of data using the FAS followed the principles of ITT.), Safety Analysis
Set (all patients who received at least one dose of study treatment), and PK Analysis Set (all
patients who received at least one dose of camizestrant, for whom there was at least
one reportable PK concentration).

Reference ID: 5864804

Statistical Methodology and Multiple Testing Procedure: Approximately 300 patients (150 in
each arm) were planned to be randomly assigned to study treatment to achieve 195 events
needed for the primary analysis of PFS. The study is powered for both PFS and the key
secondary endpoint PFS2. For PFS, assuming a 2-sided significance level of 5%,
1:1 randomization, a total of 195 PFS events provides 93% power to detect a treatment effect of
an average HR of 0.61 in the FAS (translating to an approximate improvement in median PFS
over AI + CDK4/6i from 7 to 11.5 months). For PFS2, a total of 158 PFS2 events provides
77% power to detect a treatment effect of an average HR of 0.65 in the FAS (translating to an
approximate improvement in median PFS2 over AI + CDK4/6i from 15 to 23 months).
PFS was analyzed using a stratified log-rank test adjusting for the stratification factors. The
PFS2 and OS were analyzed using identical methods as for PFS and adjusting for the same set of
stratification factors subject to the pooling strategy described in the primary analysis of the
primary endpoint. To preserve the overall type I error (family-wise error rate) at 5% in the strong
sense, an MTP including the primary and key secondary endpoints is implemented at DCO1,
DCO2, DCO3 and DCO4 (OS only) (Figure 7).
For all planned analyses of PFS, PFS2 and OS the Haybittle-Peto boundary is used to ensure
maintenance of an overall 2-sided type I error. No multiplicity adjustment will be applied for
other endpoints as other endpoints will be considered supportive endpoints.

Applicant: Multiple Testing Procedure

Interim analysis for PFS (DCO1): The DCO date was determined once the target number of PFS events
(approximately 135 events [45% maturity]) was reached, but enrollment, follow-up time after last patient
randomized, and time between interim analysis and final analysis were also considered, as per protocol, and the
DCO date was scheduled after sufficient data were collected for all patients.
Primary analysis for PFS (DCO2): The DCO for the primary analysis of PFS (DCO2) is expected to occur when
approximately 195 events [65% maturity] have been observed.
Primary analysis for PFS2 (DCO3): The DCO for the primary second progression-free survival (PFS2) analysis
(DCO3) will occur at 158 PFS2 events.
Primary analysis for OS (DCO4): The DCO for the overall survival (OS) final analysis (DCO4) will occur at 165 OS
events (55% maturity).

Sensitivity and Subgroup Analyses of PFS: For PFS, various other supplementary
(evaluation-time bias, deviation bias, censoring subjects after new anti-cancer therapy in absence
of progression or death [for both primary analyses and BICR], accounting for COVID-19 deaths)
and sensitivity (stratification according to eCRF and confounding bias) analyses were also
performed to assess the robustness of the primary endpoint. Subgroup analyses were conducted,

comparing PFS (per RECIST 1.1 using investigator assessments) between camizestrant plus

camizestrant in the following subgroups of the FAS (but not limited to): disease site (visceral

The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s description of the statistical analysis plan (SAP).
The FDA notes that additional data cutoffs occurred during the review. On June 30, 2025, DCO2
occurred at 205 PFS events. On January 2, 2026, DCO3 occurred at 170 PFS2 events. DCO4 is
not anticipated until 2028.
The FDA also adds the following clarifications:
•

•

•

OS was a key secondary endpoint. The trial was designed such that, at DCO4 (final
analysis of OS), 165 deaths are expected and would provide approximately 63% power to
detect a true treatment effect on OS, assuming a hazard ratio of 0.70 and median OS of
50 months on the camizestrant and CDK4/6i arm and 35 months in the AI and CDK4/6i
arm.
For efficacy analyses, the same stratification factors used for randomization were
prespecified to be used for the stratified analyses:
o Disease site: visceral disease versus non-visceral disease
o ESR1m detectable at first ctDNA test versus subsequent ctDNA tests
o Time from initiation of AI and CDK4/6i to randomization: <18 months versus
≥18 months (<78 weeks versus ≥78 weeks)
o CDK4/6i: palbociclib versus abemaciclib versus ribociclib
The Applicant prespecified a pooling strategy for sparse strata. For the PFS analysis at
DCO1 and the PFS2 analysis at DCO3, the CDK4/6i strata were excluded from analysis.
For the OS analysis at DCO3, the CDK4/6i strata and the disease site strata were
excluded from analysis.

The original study protocol (version 1.0 dated 25 March 2021) has been amended four times.

primary analysis of PFS2 would be based on local standard clinical practice and added
progression based on RECIST as a supplementary analysis.

Patient Disposition
Data:
SERENA-6 is a global study in which 3325 patients were screened in 264 centers in 23 countries
worldwide. A total of 406 patients entered into STEP 2 screening, of whom 315 patients were
randomized in a 1:1 ratio to receive the study treatment of either camizestrant + CDK4/6i
(N = 157) or AI + CDK4/6i (N = 158). Overall in the study, amongst the different CDK4/6i, the
proportion of patients randomized to palbociclib were (~75.0%), followed by ribociclib
(~15.0%) and abemaciclib (~10.0%); this was consistent with the real-world usage pattern of the
CDK4/6i at the time of study enrollment (Chamorro et al 2023, Luyendijk et al 2023).
At the time of DCO1, treatment was ongoing for 49.3% of patients in the camizestrant +
CDK4/6i arm and 28.9% of patients in the AI + CDK4/6i arm. Across both treatment arms, the
most common reason for study treatment discontinuation was objective disease progression.
The Applicant’s Position:
Overall, patients enrolled in the SERENA-6 study were representative of the intended population
for the study, as defined by the eligibility criteria. The number and reasons for discontinuations
from treatment did not raise any concerns about the conduct of the study.
The FDA’s Assessment:
180
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

The FDA does not agree with the Applicant’s characterization of CDK4/6i use on SERENA-6.
The trial originally only allowed patients who were receiving palbociclib to enroll. Later
abemaciclib and ribociclib were added as options on the trial. Therefore, the population enrolled
included more patients who were receiving palbociclib. Furthermore, the FDA notes that use of
ribociclib is increasing, perhaps due to demonstration of OS benefit for first-line treatment of
HR+HER2- MBC (Ribociclib USPI). The pattern of CDK4/6i use on SERENA-6 does not
represent current patterns of CDK4/6i use in the United States.
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment that relatively few patients enrolled in Step 1
of SERENA-6 were randomized in Step 2; reasons for patients not being randomized in Step 2
included no detection of an ESR1m, no ESR1m test available, radiographic progression, lack or
withdrawal of patient consent, and not meeting the eligibility criteria for Step 2. Only 9%
(315/3,325) of screened patients underwent randomization in Step 2.
Protocol Violations/Deviations
Data:
Important protocol deviations were reported in a similar proportion of patients across both
treatment arms (17.2% in the camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm and 17.1% in the AI + CDK4/6i arm)
and these were primarily related to deviations related to study procedure and prohibited
medications taken.
The Applicant’s Position:
The protocol deviations observed in the study did not raise any concerns regarding the overall
conduct or quality of the study.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of important protocol violations and
deviations. Most deviations were due to concomitant administration of QTc interval prolonging
medications [10 (6%) for the camizestrant and CDK4/6i arm and 7 (4.4%) for the AI and
CDK4/6i arm] and scans out of window [10 (6%) for the camizestrant and CDK4/6i arm and 5
(3.2%) for the AI and CDK4/6i arm]. One patient randomized to the camizestrant and CDK4/6i
arm was dispensed a kit containing an AI for cycle 6. Ultimately, these deviations are unlikely to
have substantially affected the results of SERENA-6.

Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
Data and Applicant’s Position:

about the conduct of the study. Overall, the concomitant treatments administered were

considered to have impacted the study results. The use of allowed concomitant medication was
balanced across both treatment arms.

medications, and rescue medications with the following clarifications. See FDA review of
protocol deviations above in “Protocol Deviations/Violations” for details regarding QTc interval
prolonging medications. Also, patients on the camizestrant and CDK4/6i arm were more likely to
be taking opioids in combination with non-opioid analgesics than patients on the AI and
CDK4/6i arm (12% versus 6%).

Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

The Applicant’s proposed indication represents a new treatment paradigm which
intertwines the treatment effect of camizestrant with an experimental treatment
strategy that is not proven to have long-term clinical benefit.
• The FDA remains uncertain as to whether the intended patients would benefit from
the proposed experimental strategy of receiving a new treatment (i.e., camizestrant) at
detection of an ESR1m compared to receiving it at radiographic progression as
SERENA-6 was not designed to assess this and FDA is not aware of any external data
supporting this either. Thus, it is unclear whether the experimental treatment strategy
would result in long-term benefit for patients in this incurable disease setting
2. The clinical meaningfulness of the PFS improvement measured from ESR1m detection is
uncertain.
• Measurement of PFS in SERENA-6 starts at a new time point, upon detection of an
ESR1m, which initiates randomization. This is not the same PFS starting point (e.g.,
diagnosis for first-line trials or radiographic progression for second-line trials) that
has been used to support the clinical benefit of other drugs for patients with
HR+HER2- MBC. Consequently, the FDA is uncertain whether the observed
improvement in PFS in this trial is clinically meaningful. By switching therapy early,
patients may not be maximizing each line of therapy. Patients on the control arm who
did not change treatments had an estimated median PFS of 9.2 months (95% CI: 7.2,
9.5).

The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment.

Reference ID: 5864804

Abbreviations: AI, aromatase inhibitor; CDK4/6i, cyclin-dependent kinases 4 and 6 inhibitor; CI, confidence
interval; DCO, data cutoff; n, number of events; N, number of patients in the treatment group; NC, noncomputable;
OS, overall survival; PFS2, second progression-free survival

PFS2
At DCO3, the PFS2 results reached statistical significance with a HR of 0.63 (95% CI: 0.46,
0.86). PFS2 was defined as the time from randomization, after ESR1m detection, until the second
progression (which could have been clinical progression or radiographic progression, whether
meeting RECIST criteria or not).
The FDA does not consider PFS2 interpretable as a measure of clinical benefit. PFS2 is not
typically used for regulatory decision-making as it does not isolate the effect of the experimental
drug. Additionally, the choice of subsequent therapy was left to clinicians’ discretion which led
to heterogeneity, including a higher number of patients on the camizestrant and CDK4/6i arm
receiving chemotherapy or an ADC as their next therapy compared to the AI and CDK4/6i arm
(44% versus 31%) at DCO3. Relatively few patients on the control arm received an oral estrogen
receptor antagonist as their next therapy. No crossover to camizestrant or camizestrant and
CDK4/6i was allowed, which would have allowed some characterization of the effect of
switching at ESR1m rather than at radiographic progression.
Further, the FDA disagrees that PFS2 substantiates the benefit of an early switch to camizestrant.
At PFS2, patients had received two new therapies on the investigational arm and only one new
therapy on the control arm, but there was no assessment of the timing of the switch to first new
therapy and whether it would be better to change therapies at ESR1m detection or at radiographic
progression.
In SERENA-6, investigators were allowed to determine a PFS2 event by one or more of the
following: clinical progression, unequivocal radiological progression, and RECIST v1.1
progression. FDA sent an information request to the Applicant on March 31, 2026, seeking
clarification on what constituted clinical progression and unequivocal radiological progression.
According to the Applicant response, an investigator could determine clinical progression based
on subjective disease progression considering symptomatic deterioration and clinical context.
Unequivocal progression was assessed by investigator based on overall impression of the
radiological assessment without a need to follow RECIST v1.1. Clinicians were also allowed to
use imaging modalities other than CT or MRI based on local practice, further increasing
heterogeneity and uncertainty around this endpoint. These options for determining a PFS2 event
introduce subjectivity to the PFS2 determination and make the PFS2 results more unreliable.
There was no BICR sensitivity analysis for PFS2.

Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)

Reference ID: 5864804

Regarding chemotherapy-free survival, FDA acknowledges that the transition from ET to
chemotherapy may be clinically important in an individual patient’s disease course. However,
FDA does not consider chemotherapy/ADC-free survival suitable for evaluating clinical benefit
to support regulatory decision-making. Chemotherapy/ADC-free survival is defined as time from
randomization until chemotherapy or ADC initiation, which reflects a clinical decision rather
than a direct assessment of disease status or patient benefit. Furthermore, the decision to initiate
chemotherapy or an ADC may vary based on drug availability, clinician and patient preference,
and clinical setting, which introduces heterogeneity into the endpoint and complicates attribution
of any improvement in this endpoint to the drug. Additionally, FDA notes that more patients
received chemotherapy or an ADC as subsequent therapy on the camizestrant and CDK4/6i arm
than on the AI and CDK4/6i arm (44% versus 31%).
Dose/Dose Response
The Applicant’s Position:
Not applicable as only one dose was studied.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant that only one dose was studied. However, one dose
reduction was allowed on SERENA-6. The Applicant is not proposing to market more than one
dosage of camizestrant but proposes dosage interruptions to address adverse events. For more
information regarding dosage modifications on SERENA-6, refer to Section 8.2.
Durability of Response
Data and The Applicant’s Position:
The number of patients with confirmed response was very low (assessed from ORR seen in
11 patients in the camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm and 3 patients in the AI + CDK4/6i arm). The
ORR was low because the patients were clinically benefiting from the AI + CDK4/6i treatment
that they were receiving for ≥ 6 months prior to randomization. The median DoR in camizestrant
+ CDK4/6i arm was 19.35 months compared to the AI + CDK4/6i arm where it was
15.97 months.
The FDA’s Assessment:

The FDA agrees that confirmed ORR was low on both arms. However, it is notable that 3
patients on the control arm had confirmed responses since they continued on the same therapy
after randomization. The FDA disagrees with the Applicant that duration of response is
interpretable as a reliable measure of clinical benefit since it is a responder analysis, and
randomization is not preserved.
Persistence of Effect
Data:
The persistent effect of camizestrant at 75 mg QD has been discussed in the previous efficacy
results sections including DoR and PFS as well as in Section 6.3.1.
The Applicant’s Position:
The oncology endpoints in the pivotal SERENA-6 study are based on time-to-event analyses, so
persistence of efficacy is inherent in the endpoint measurements. The data obtained to date
provide evidence that camizestrant + CDK4/6i offers a clinically meaningful extension of PFS
benefit in the patient population treated.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA does not agree that SERENA-6 demonstrated a clinical meaningful improvement in
PFS for the intended patient population. Refer to Section 8.1.6 for FDA’s comprehensive
assessment.
Efficacy Results – Secondary or Exploratory COA (PRO) Endpoints
Data:
A clinically meaningful reduction in the risk of TTD in GHS/QoL was observed in the
camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm compared to the AI + CDK4/6i arm (HR = 0.53; 95% CI: 0.33,
0.82, with a prolonged median TTD [23.03 months in camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm vs
6.37 months in AI + CDK4/6i arm]).
A clinically meaningful reduction in the risk of TTD in pain was observed in the camizestrant +
CDK4/6i arm compared to the AI + CDK4/6i arm (HR = 0.57; 95% CI: 0.37, 0.86, with a
200
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

TTD estimates, particularly for later timepoints where the Kaplan-Meier curves
may be driven by a small number of patients remaining at risk.
c. Sparse Assessment Frequency: PRO assessment frequency was sparse after Week
12, occurring only every 8 weeks. An infrequent assessment schedule makes
capture of deterioration events challenging and the results less reliable.
3. PRO baseline assessment timing: In SERENA-6, the baseline assessment occurs at
ESR1m detection, which represents a molecular event rather than clinical or radiographic
disease progression. For both TTD and change from baseline PRO analyses, a baseline
assessment is an important relative milestone. Usually, this baseline assessment occurs at
a time of disease progression or before initiation of a new therapy with the expectation
that improvements in disease symptoms will occur and patient’s functioning will change
due to the treatment being studied. In SERENA-6, baseline at ESR1m detection is not at a
timepoint of disease progression and makes any PRO result relative to baseline difficult
to interpret. Patients may also have varying underlying symptoms from ongoing
treatment, making this even more unreliable and heterogenous.
4. Marginal Magnitude of Change from Baseline Analyses: Despite the arbitrary nature of
the baseline timepoint assessment as described above, FDA examined whether there was
improvement in PRO scores to support the Applicant’s claims. For physical functioning,
the mean change from baseline at Week 28 was -0.18 in the camizestrant and CDK4/6
inhibitor arm versus -2.95 in the AI and CDK4/6 inhibitor arm—a difference of smaller
than 3 points on a 100-point scale. For pain, the mean change from baseline at Week 28
was 0.67 in the camizestrant and CDK4/6 inhibitor arm versus 6.59 in the AI and
CDK4/6 inhibitor arm. While these numerical differences favor camizestrant, the
magnitude of difference is small and of uncertain clinical meaningfulness. Furthermore,
these analyses are compromised by differential missing data between arms, with
consistently lower completion rates in the control arm at later timepoints. The analysis
assumes data are missing at random, which is not supported by the differential disease
progression rates between arms, potentially biasing results.
5. Although the Applicant considered some PRO-based endpoints as secondary endpoints,
FDA considered all PRO-based endpoints as exploratory as they were not controlled for
multiplicity. Without appropriate control for multiple comparisons, the observed
numerical trends cannot be considered evidence of benefit.
FDA does not consider the SERENA-6 PRO results as evidence in support of clinical benefit.
The TTD endpoint results are unreliable for the reasons discussed above, and the change from
baseline improvements are marginal and of uncertain clinical significance especially relative to a

multiplicity control, preclude the use of these PRO data to support a claim of clinical benefit for

Additional Analyses Conducted on the Individual Trial
The Applicant’s Position:
No additional analyses were conducted.
The FDA’s Assessment:
As part of the Point/Counterpoint briefing document prepared for the Oncology Drug Advisory
Committee (ODAC) meeting (refer to Section 9), the Applicant included some information on
ESR1m ctDNA from SERENA-6. On May 14, 2026, during an informal teleconference, the FDA
agreed to review additional exploratory ctDNA analyses from the Applicant. The information
included by the Applicant in the briefing document and in information amendments submitted on
May 21, 2026, and on June 8, 2026, is summarized below, along with the FDA’s assessment of
this information.
The Applicant submitted exploratory analyses of ctDNA dynamics from SERENA-6 and
SERENA-2, including analyses of clearance of total ctDNA (clearance rate and percent change
for both trials) and ESR1m in ctDNA (clearance rate and percent change for SERENA-6,
clearance rate for SERENA-2) along with associations between ctDNA changes and immature
OS data. To justify the clinical relevance of ctDNA dynamics, they also included a summary of
selected exploratory analyses of ctDNA changes along with iDFS, PFS and/or OS from eight
clinical trials in early stage or advanced/metastatic HR+HER2- breast cancer as well as other
solid tumors.
The FDA does not agree that the eight selected trials in ER+HER2- breast cancer support ctDNA
reduction or clearance as a clinically meaningful endpoint. The trials differed notably in ctDNA
endpoint definition, method of assessment, timepoint, and mechanism of the drug under
evaluation. Each trial also likely had missing assessments, but the number of missing
assessments is not available from the Applicant’s analysis. In the metastatic trials, PFS was
assessed from randomization at disease progression which is different from SERENA-6. Pooling
of the ctDNA information across trials would be inappropriate due to the differences mentioned,
and the Applicant did not conduct a pooled analysis to evaluate for patient-level or trial-level
associations between ctDNA changes and endpoints used for regulatory decision-making.
205
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

Finally, these analyses were not controlled for multiplicity and are unable to serve as a basis for
valid statistical inference that ctDNA changes are associated with clinically meaningful longterm outcomes.
In SERENA-6, blood collection for ctDNA analysis occurred pre-treatment, at Cycle 2 Day 1,
and at Cycle 3 Day 1. The Guardant 360 assay was used to test for ctDNA. The FDA does not
believe these results are supportive of clinical benefit for several reasons. The data may be
unreliable because patients had varying amounts of ctDNA and repeat testing may have
generated ctDNA changes based on assay sensitivity in the absence of a clinical change. The
Applicant also claims that clearance on the AI and CDK4/6i arm is due to the limit of detection
of the test, which is unverifiable. Additionally, a substantial proportion of patients enrolled in
SERENA-6 are not included in the total ctDNA clearance analyses. Out of a total of 315 patients,
only 188 patients had paired ctDNA results at Cycle 2 Day 1 and 172 patients at Cycle 3 Day 1.
More patients were evaluable for clearance of ESR1m, but some patients were still missing
paired assessments. Data for these patients may not be missing at random, further complicating
interpretation of the results.
Importantly, the clinical meaningfulness of ctDNA changes (total or ESR1m) at the selected time
points have not been established. Whether ctDNA (total or ESR1m) clearance, reduction, or both
are clinically meaningful at any given timepoint is uncertain. It is also challenging to conclude
that there is an association between the reported ctDNA changes and OS as presented by the
Applicant due to the issues with ctDNA reliability discussed above and the immaturity of the OS
information. Finally, all ctDNA analyses were not included in the multiplicity control plan for
SERENA-6.
SERENA-2 is an exploratory trial because false positive findings were not controlled to the
regulatory standard of a two-sided alpha of 0.05. In SERENA-2, blood collection for ctDNA
analysis also occurred pre-treatment, at Cycle 2 Day 1, and at Cycle 3 Day 1. Total ctDNA and
ESR1m analyses and associations with OS have similar limitations as for SERENA-6, including
challenges with missing data. Out of 220 patients, only 135 had paired total ctDNA results at
Cycle 2 Day 1 and only 92 at Cycle 3 Day 1. Even fewer patients had paired samples for ESR1m.
The Applicant also compares ctDNA data from SERENA-6 and SERENA-2. They conclude that
because more patients had clearance of ctDNA and ESR1m with camizestrant in SERENA-6
compared to in SERENA-2, this supports an early switch strategy. FDA does not consider this
cross-trial comparison, which does not even include all patients randomized to either trial,
interpretable. Variability in baseline demographics and disease characteristics could lead to
differences in the treatment effect observed across trials. Additionally, patients on SERENA-6
received camizestrant and CDK4/6i and patients in SERENA-2 received camizestrant alone.

In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Most importantly, as previously stated, the relationship of ctDNA clearance to any clinically
relevant outcome measure is unknown. Overall, the FDA concludes that the ctDNA results of
SERENA-2 and SERENA-6 cannot support a benefit for switching therapy at detection of a
ESR1m rather than radiographic progression.
Supportive Study: SERENA-2 (D8530C00002)
SERENA-2 is an open-label, randomized Phase II study evaluating the efficacy and safety of
camizestrant (75, 150, and 300 mg, oral QD) as a monotherapy in comparison with fulvestrant
administered according to its label in post-menopausal women with advanced ER-positive,
HER2-negative breast cancer. An overview of the SERENA-2 study is presented in Table 12.
Prior chemotherapy and/or ET was permitted, albeit with no more than one line of chemotherapy
and one line of ET for advanced disease. Prior treatment with CDK4/6i was permitted.
During study conduct, review of the SERENA-1 Phase I camizestrant monotherapy data revealed
no increment in efficacy with increases of dose above 150 mg. Based on this information, the
sponsor closed further recruitment to the 300 mg arm of SERENA-2 on 21 December 2020, and
hence these data are not presented.

patients treated with 75 mg QD or 150 mg QD camizestrant as a monotherapy in SERENA-2 are
presented in Table 25.

In patients previously treated with CDK4/6i, camizestrant enhances PFS to 5.5 months [90% CI:

Camizestrant demonstrated clinical efficacy as a monotherapy over fulvestrant in pre-treated
patients with endocrine-sensitive ABC disease, including those who had progressed following
CDK4/6i therapy, or had detectable ESR1m.

SERENA-2 is an exploratory trial because false positive findings (i.e., Type I error) were not
controlled at the regulatory standard of a two-sided alpha of 0.05. FDA considers any results
hypothesis generating only and not sufficient to establish the superiority of camizestrant. The
FDA disagrees with the Applicant’s characterization of the p-values for PFS as “statistically
significant” because formal comparisons were neither prespecified nor performed in SERENA-2.
FDA does believe that the exploratory results from SERENA-2 show that camizestrant has
activity in patients with HR+HER- MBC, including those with ESR1m.
An additional trial, SERENA-4, evaluating camizestrant in for treatment of HR+HER2- MBC is
also ongoing. This is a randomized (1:1), multicenter, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial with
registrational intent in patients with ER+HER2- MBC who have not received previous systemic
treatment for metastatic disease (first-line MBC setting). Eligible patients are randomly assigned
to receive either camizestrant and palbociclib or anastrozole (an AI) and palbociclib. The
primary endpoint is investigator assessed PFS and OS is a key secondary endpoint. PFS by BICR
is an exploratory endpoint. Although the FDA does not believe that this trial could be used to
support an indication for treatment of patients with HR+HER2- MBC upon detection of an
ESR1m based on its design, it may show some evidence of camizestrant activity. The FDA sent
an information request on May 11, 2026, requesting that the Applicant provide an update on the
status of this trial. In a written response received on May 12, 2026, the Applicant stated that the
target number of PFS events for the primary analysis is 819 and as of May 4, 2026, the observed
number of PFS events is 826. The Applicant stated that with a data cutoff date in June 2026, the
final report submission would occur in Q4 2026. It is not clear to FDA why there may be a sixmonth lag between the data cutoff date and availability of PFS results. The FDA notes that two
other similar trials evaluating an oral estrogen receptor antagonist in combination with
palbociclib compared to an AI and palbociclib failed to reach their primary PFS endpoints
(Turner et al 2026 and Cortes et al 2024).

Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness
Data:
All relevant efficacy data are described in Section 8.1.2.
The Applicant’s Position:
SERENA-6 demonstrated clinical benefit for endpoints which are well accepted by Regulatory
Authorities worldwide to evaluate efficacy and safety outcomes in the breast cancer population.
The SERENA-6 results demonstrate that camizestrant + CDK4/6i guided by emergence of
ESR1m during 1L AI + CDK4/6i in patients with hormone receptor-positive, HER2-negative
ABC resulted in a statistically significant and clinically meaningful improvement in investigatorassessed PFS vs AI + CDK4/6i (HR = 0.44; 95% CI: 0.31, 0.60; p < 0.00001), which was further
supported by BICR-assessed PFS (HR 0.43; 95% CI: 0.29, 0.63; nominal p = 0.00001). The PFS
benefit in the camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm was maintained throughout the study, as evidenced
by the larger proportion of patients estimated to be alive and progression-free at 6 months
(83.2% of patients in the camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm vs 62.4% of patients in the AI + CDK4/6i
arm), 12 months (60.7% of patients vs 33.4% patients, respectively), or 24 months (29.7% of
patients vs 5.4% of patients, respectively). The robustness of the primary analysis was also
210

In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

confirmed by the results of the predefined sensitivity analyses (including stratification factors
and adjusting for potential confounding factors). Importantly, the benefit was observed
consistently across pre-specified subgroups, irrespective of the choice of the CDK4/6i
(palbociclib, ribociclib, or abemaciclib). Treatment benefit was also consistently observed in
patients with single or multiple ESR1m variants and in patients with detectable levels of each of
the 3 most prevalent ESR1m variants.
Beyond initial treatment, the camizestrant + CDK4/6i also reduced PFS2 risk by 48% (HR 0.52;
95% CI: 0.33, 0.81; p = 0.00376, not statistically significant [pre-specified statistical significance
level = 0.0001]), showing persistent and sustained benefit through the next line of therapy.
Additionally, treatment with camizestrant + CDK4/6i also delayed time to first and second
subsequent therapy, indicating the PFS benefit was largely preserved through subsequent lines of
therapy, supporting the long-term benefit. At the time of the interim PFS analysis (DCO1), the
OS data at 12.4% maturity with 39 events showed a numerical trend favoring the camizestrant
arm (HR 0.91; 95% CI: 0.48, 1.73). Continuation of OS follow-up is planned until 55% maturity
(165 events).
PROs complemented these results, showing a 47% reduction in deterioration of GHS/QoL and a
43% reduction in pain deterioration risk. These findings support the overall clinical benefit of the
treatment with camizestrant and provide evidence on the benefit-risk profile from a patient’s
perspective.
Overall, these findings suggest that switching 1L ET to camizestrant, while continuing CDK4/6i
upon detection of ESR1m and ahead of disease progression, extends the benefit of 1L treatment
in patients whose disease develops molecular resistance to AI and substantially delays disease
progression.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA believes there is uncertainty in clinical benefit for camizestrant based on the results of
SERENA-6 despite a statistically significant improvement in PFS and PFS2. SERENA-6 was not
designed to demonstrate that switching therapy at ESR1m detection is better than switching
therapy at radiographic progression. Although FDA uses PFS to support regular approvals in
HR+HER2- MBC, the PFS endpoint employed in SERENA-6 was different, because
randomization occurred at ESR1m detection. Whether an estimated 6.8-month median PFS
improvement measured from detection of an ESR1m constitutes clinical benefit is uncertain.
PFS2 results were considered uninterpretable as evidence of benefit. The OS results are currently
immature and have not achieved statistical significance; it is unknown whether they will do so at
the time of final analysis. Other exploratory endpoints in SERENA-6 (landmark PFS analyses,

Reference ID: 5864804

chemotherapy/ADC-free survival, time to subsequent therapy, and PROs) were not alphacontrolled for multiplicity and cannot be used to support the finding of clinical benefit.
FDA notes the following key review issues and has several disagreements with the Applicant.
For more detail on all issues raised, refer to Section 8.1.2.
1. Trial Design
a. The FDA disagrees that the intended patients would benefit from the proposed
experimental strategy of receiving a new treatment (i.e., camizestrant) at detection
of an ESR1m compared to receiving it at radiographic progression, because
SERENA-6 was not designed to assess this clinical question. There are no
external data to support this therapy switch strategy, either. The absence of a
comparator arm in which patients switch at radiographic progression means that
the trial cannot establish whether earlier switching confers any advantage over the
standard approach. Thus, it is unclear whether the experimental treatment strategy
would result in long-term benefit for patients in this incurable disease setting.
b. Neither PFS nor PFS2 is adequate to demonstrate clinical benefit of camizestrant.
For both endpoints, patients on the two arms have received different numbers of
new treatment regimens after randomization, without any assessment of whether
switching at ESR1m is beneficial compared with switching at radiographic
progression. This structural imbalance in treatments between arms fundamentally
undermines the interpretability of both endpoints as measures of the benefit
attributable to camizestrant.
c. The Applicant’s proposed indication “upon emergence of an ESR1m” is not
consistent with the population that was enrolled, because patients did not have to
have a negative test before enrolling and around half of the patients had an
ESR1m detected on the first test. This raises questions about whether the proposed
indication accurately characterizes the enrolled population and whether the trial
results can be generalized to patients who truly have emergent ESR1m during
treatment.
2. PFS Interpretation
a. FDA disagrees with the Applicant that PFS in SERENA-6 is the same as PFS in
other trials that supported approval of drugs in early ER+HER2- MBC.
Measurement of PFS in SERENA-6 starts at a new time point, upon detection of
an ESR1m, which initiates randomization. In other trials, randomization occurred
at a time when a new therapy was clearly needed (e.g., after diagnosis or
212
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

progression on the current therapy). This fundamental difference in the starting
point for PFS measurement means that the observed PFS improvement in
SERENA-6 cannot be interpreted in the same manner as PFS improvements in
prior trials, and the clinical meaningfulness of the observed result remains
uncertain. By switching therapy early, patients may not be maximizing the benefit
of each line of therapy—as evidenced by the estimated 9.2-month (95% CI: 7.2,
9.5) median PFS experienced by patients on the control arm. Patients may be
stopping a well-tolerated therapy early with unknown consequences on long-term
outcomes.
b. The FDA acknowledges that the Applicant has presented Kaplan-Meier estimates
of PFS rates at various landmark time points. However, the landmark PFS rates
do not resolve the issue of a new starting point that complicates interpretation.
Furthermore, even when defined using a conventional starting point, FDA
generally interprets landmark analyses with caution, because they may
misrepresent clinical benefit by focusing on specific timepoints rather than the
full time-to-event distribution. The estimates of PFS rates at any given time point
would also be impacted by censoring and duration of follow-up and may not be
robust at later time points.
c. Elsewhere in the submission (Section 2.2), the Applicant suggests that an early
switch approach such as SERENA-6 is beneficial by citing data from
monotherapy trials of oral estrogen receptor antagonists showing limited activity
in the 2L setting. FDA disagrees that the estimated median PFS results from
monotherapy trials of oral estrogen receptor antagonists can be used to support
clinical benefit for switching at ESR1m detection because these cross-trial
comparisons are rendered uninterpretable by confounding factors and do not
account for the contribution of a CDK4/6i. Reliance on such cross-trial
comparisons to justify a novel treatment strategy is not an adequate substitute for
a trial designed to directly test that strategy.
3. PFS2 Endpoint and Interpretation
a. FDA does not believe that PFS2 is interpretable as a measure of clinical benefit,
as communicated to the Applicant on November 4, 2024, in the Final Written
Response to a Type C meeting request. In general, PFS2 does not isolate the
effect of the investigational drug since it evaluates the combined effect of the
randomized treatment and subsequent therapy, rather than the effect of the
initially randomized treatment. Consequently, any observed difference in PFS2
between the treatment arms cannot be attributed to a specific treatment
component, as the endpoint definition does not allow the effect of any individual
213
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

therapy to be isolated and is not evidence of any sustained benefit attributable to
camizestrant.
b. Despite FDA’s position that PFS2 is not interpretable as a measure of clinical
benefit in trials such as SERENA-6, we did review the results and note the
following additional issues with interpreting the results:
i. PFS2 also starts at randomization after detection of an ESR1m and is
unable to demonstrate a benefit for switching to a new therapy early
versus switching at radiographic progression.
ii. SERENA-6 did not allow patients on the control arm to cross over to
camizestrant and a CDK4/6i after radiographic progression. For
subsequent therapy, the timing and choice was at the investigator’s
discretion. The SERENA-6 protocol instructed investigators to follow the
most recent version of several different internationally recognized
guidelines. However, variability in regional availability of drugs and local
standards created heterogeneity in subsequent therapy with patients
receiving one of many different treatment regimens, which further impacts
the interpretability of the results. A larger proportion of patients on the
investigational arm of SERENA-6 (44%) received chemotherapy or an
ADC (versus 31% on the control arm) as their first subsequent therapy.
Despite the whole population having tumors with an ESR1m, relatively
few patients on either arm received an oral estrogen receptor antagonist in
combinations or as monotherapy (2.2% on the investigational arm versus
13% on the control arm). This imbalance in subsequent therapy further
confounds the PFS2 results and precludes attribution of any observed
difference to camizestrant.
iii. In SERENA-6, investigators were allowed to determine a PFS2 event by
one or more of the following: clinical progression, unequivocal
radiological progression, and RECIST v1.1 progression. There was no
BICR sensitivity analysis for PFS2. This is further summarized in Section
8.1.2.
4. OS Results
a. The FDA agrees that the current OS data at DCO3 are immature at 58%
information fraction (with 95 of the 165 OS events required for final analysis
observed) and have not achieved statistical significance. Final OS results are not
expected until 2028 (as communicated by the Applicant to FDA in an information
214
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

request response on March 30, 2026). In the absence of mature OS data, there is
no evidence that the early therapy-switch strategy employed in SERENA-6
translates into a long-term survival benefit for patients.
b. The FDA does not typically require demonstration of a statistically significant OS
improvement for approvals in HR+HER2- MBC. However, in this case, given the
significant uncertainties surrounding the trial design, the novel and unvalidated
starting point for PFS measurement, and the lack of a direct comparison of
switching strategies, achieving a statistically significant improvement in OS could
overcome some of the uncertainties with the SERENA-6 trial design and clinical
meaningfulness of the PFS result.
5. Other Exploratory Endpoints
a. PROs (Time to Deterioration): FDA does not agree that the SERENA-6 PRO
results support clinical benefit. TTD endpoints from SERENA-6 are unreliable
due to infrequent events, the schedule of collection, poor data quality, uncertain
clinical meaningfulness, and minimal changes that were observed relative to an
arbitrary baseline (ESR1 mutation detection). The choice of ESR1m detection as
the baseline for PRO assessment is particularly problematic, as patients at this
time point are typically asymptomatic, making it difficult to detect or interpret
any meaningful change in patient-reported outcomes. PRO-based endpoints in
SERENA-6 were not controlled for multiplicity and results must be interpreted
with caution.
b. Chemotherapy/ADC-free survival and time to subsequent therapy: Other time to
event endpoints are unreliable as a measure of clinical benefit because they do not
provide a direct assessment of disease status or patient benefit. Rather they
represent a clinical decision that is influenced by a variety of factors, including
drug availability, clinician and patient preference, and clinical setting.
Furthermore, these endpoints are particularly susceptible to bias in a trial such as
SERENA-6, where subsequent therapy was highly heterogeneous and not
protocol-specified. Additionally, these analyses were not included in the
multiplicity control planning.
c. ctDNA analyses were limited by lack of control for multiplicity, missing data,
assay sensitivity, uncertain clinical relevance of the timepoints chosen for
assessment, and unclear clinical meaningfulness in terms of their relationship to
important long-term outcomes such as OS. ctDNA response data cannot substitute
for validated clinical endpoints and do not provide an adequate basis for
concluding that camizestrant confers meaningful clinical benefit.

Overall, even with a positive PFS finding, the FDA review team feels that there is uncertainty in
clinical benefit for camizestrant based on the results of SERENA-6. Further verification of
clinical benefit is needed, for which two PMRs will be issued. As detailed above, FDA’s main
concern is that the benefit of a early treatment switch strategy compared to a standard treatment
switch strategy has not been established. The first PMR outlines a new trial which will compare
the early-switch approach to a standard-switch approach using a time-to-strategy-failure (TSF)
endpoint and is designed to address this uncertainty. The second PMR is for final OS results
from SERENA-6. As noted above, a statistically significant improvement in OS could overcome
some of the uncertainties regarding clinical benefit in SERENA-6.

Review of Safety
The Applicant’s Position:
See individual sections below.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA’s safety assessment is based primarily on independent review of data from the
SERENA-6 trial. The FDA’s safety analysis focused on deaths and treatment emergent adverse
events (TEAEs) including serious adverse events (SAEs), TEAEs leading to trial drug
discontinuation, dose reduction, or dosage interruption, grade 3-4 TEAEs, and all-grade TEAEs.
The FDA also reviewed laboratory data. AEs of special interest identified by the Applicant
included visual effects (grouped term-GT) and bradycardia. The FDA prefers use of visual
disturbances as the GT rather than visual effects since it better characterizes the individual
preferred terms (PT) as further discussed in Section 8.2.5. The FDA identified QTc interval
prolongation as an additional important safety topic, particularly in combination with
bradycardia as further discussed under QT Prolongation and Risk of Arrhythmia in Section 8.2.5.
FDA’s safety review focused on better understanding the visual disturbances, bradycardia and
QTc interval prolongation associated with camizestrant.
Results for the camizestrant and CDK4/6i pooled population and camizestrant monotherapy
population (as outlined in Section 8.2.1) were also reviewed and in general, did not identify any
new safety signals when compared to the SERENA-6 trial.

Safety Review Approach
The Applicant’s Position:
The safety evaluation focuses on the safety population, ie, patients who received at least one
dose of camizestrant 75 mg QD in SERENA-6 and other studies included in the pooled data. The
pivotal analysis of the safety and tolerability data of camizestrant + CDK4/6i (palbociclib,
ribociclib, or abemaciclib) in the proposed indication is based on the pre-planned interim
analysis (DCO1) of data from the Phase III SERENA-6 study (DCO date: 28 November 2024).
Safety data are also provided from 2 supportive pooled populations:
•

•

Camizestrant + CDK4/6i combination pool: The combination pool includes safety data
from the SERENA-6 and SERENA-1 (Parts C, D, G, H, K, and L) studies evaluating the
combination of camizestrant 75 mg QD + any CDK4/6i (ie, camizestrant + palbociclib,
camizestrant + ribociclib, or camizestrant + abemaciclib). Pooled data of the combination of
camizestrant + CDK4/6i are also presented by individual CDK4/6i (ie, palbociclib, ribociclib,
or abemaciclib).
Monotherapy pool: The monotherapy pool includes camizestrant safety data from the
SERENA-1 (Parts A and B), SERENA-2, Japan and China Phase I studies.

The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the described safety patient populations. The primary safety analyses were
based on review of the SERENA-6 population. The FDA also evaluated the camizestrant and
CDK4/6i combination pooled population which consisted of 263 patients from SERENA-6 and
SERENA-1 who were treated with camizestrant 75mg daily in combination with palbociclib
(141 patients), ribociclib (83 patients) or abemaciclib (39 patients). Out of the 83 patients treated
with camizestrant and ribociclib, 28 patients received ribociclib at the 400mg dose and 32
patients received ribociclib at the 600mg dose. In SERENA-6, only a limited number of patients
in the camizestrant and CDK4/6i arm were treated with camizestrant 75mg daily in combination
with either ribociclib 600mg daily 21 days on/7 days off (23 patients or 15%) or abemaciclib
150mg twice daily (15 patients or 10%). The camizestrant and ribociclib combination is of
special interest given the increased risk for serious arrhythmia as discussed under in Section
8.2.5.3.
The Applicant is not seeking an indication for camizestrant monotherapy. The monotherapy
pooled population consisted of 117 patients from SERENA-1, SERENA-2, and Japan and China
Phase 1 trials who were treated with camizestrant 75mg daily.

Treatment duration calculation is similar to that in c but change the last dose date to the last dose of either
camizestrant or CDK4/6i, whichever occurs first and the first dose date to the first dose of camizestrant or
CDK4/6i, whichever occurs later
b
Total treatment years is defined as the sum of exposure time from all patients (source: confirmed from stats)
c
Total treatment duration (months) = (min [date of last dose date where dose > 0 + scheduled off treatment
days, date of death, date of DCO] - first dose date + 1) / (365.25/12).
d
Actual treatment duration = total intended treatment duration minus the total duration of dose interruptions
(including any planned no dose periods occurring before the planned dose is resumed).
Source: Table 2.7.4.2.1b; ISS Module 5.3.5.3.
a

The Applicant’s Position:
Consistent with longer PFS, camizestrant had a longer median intended exposure than AI (10.22
months vs 6.31 months, respectively), and the longer exposure of camizestrant was sustained
over the treatment period. The intended exposure of CDK4/6i was longer in the camizestrant +
CDK4/6i arm than in the AI + CDK4/6i arm (10.12 months vs 6.31 months, respectively).
Additionally, the intended exposure and actual treatment duration of camizestrant were similar,
indicating minimal treatment interruptions.
The exposure to both the study treatment (camizestrant + CDK4/6i) and camizestrant was longer
in the SERENA-6 study compared to the camizestrant + CDK4/6i combination pool and the
monotherapy pool. This was expected due to differences in study design and study populations;
studies in the pooled datasets included Phase I and Phase II studies in which patients were
generally more heavily pre-treated, compared to the SERENA-6 study in which patients were
receiving and benefiting from AI + CDK4/6i in 1L at study entry.
The FDA’s Assessment:

The FDA agrees that patients in the camizestrant and CDK4/6i arm of SERENA-6 had longer

Relevant Characteristics of the Safety Population:
Data:
The baseline demographic and disease characteristics of patients in the SERENA-6 study were
well balanced between the 2 treatment arms (Table 14 and Table 15), and also between the
camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm in SERENA-6 and the pooled datasets, with a few exceptions,
largely based on the nature of individual studies in the pooled dataset and the SERENA-6 study:
•
•

The proportion of patients with liver disease was lower in the SERENA-6 camizestrant +
CDK4/6i arm (16.8%) compared to the camizestrant + CDK4/6i pool (33.1%), and the
monotherapy pool (29.9%).
The proportion of patients with prior chemotherapy in advanced setting was lower (3.9%) in
the SERENA-6 camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm compared to the camizestrant + CDK4/6i pool
(19.8%), and the monotherapy pool (18.8%).

The Applicant’s Position:
Overall, in the SERENA-6 study, demographics and baseline characteristics were well balanced
between the treatment arms. All the studies in the SERENA-6 safety pools were selected to
ensure that the eligibility criteria of the study populations are relevant to the population of
patients intended for treatment in the proposed SERENA-6 indication.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees that the demographics and baseline characteristics between the two treatment
arms in SERENA-6 were generally well balanced.

The Applicant’s Position:
The evaluation of safety is based primarily on data from the SERENA-6 study, with supportive
data from the SERENA-1, SERENA-2, Japan Phase I, and China Phase I studies. These data
allow for an informed assessment of the safety profile of camizestrant + CDK4/6i and an
evaluation of the overall benefit-risk in the target population.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA notes that the numbers of patients receiving camizestrant in combination with
ribociclib and camizestrant in combination with abemaciclib were limited in SERENA-6 as well
as in the camizestrant and CDK4/6i combination safety pool (refer to Section 8.2.1). Therefore,
these combinations were not extensively evaluated. FDA disagrees that cardiac safety with the
camizestrant and ribociclib combination has been adequately characterized, as discussed under in
Section 8.2.5.3.

Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
The Applicant’s Position:
No issues relating to data integrity or quality were identified for the SERENA-6 study. The
Sponsor’s procedures, internal quality control measures, and audit programs provide reassurance
that the camizestrant clinical development program is being conducted as per GCP, as
documented by ICH.

The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment regarding data integrity and submission
quality.
Categorization of Adverse Event
The Applicant’s Position:

223
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

The study protocol defined an AE as the development of any untoward medical occurrence
(other than progression of the malignancy under evaluation) in a patient administered a study
treatment and which does not necessarily have a causal relationship with this treatment. TEAEs
for patients on combination therapy, camizestrant/AI + CDK4/6i, were defined as those AEs
with onset or that worsen (by investigator report of a change in intensity) after the first dose of
camizestrant, AI (anastrozole or letrozole), or CDK4/6i (palbociclib, ribociclib, or abemaciclib),
whichever occurred first, and on or before the date of last dose of
camizestrant/anastrozole/letrozole + 28 days. For patients on the camizestrant monotherapy,
TEAEs were defined in a similar way but excluding the AI and CDK4/6i dose. In the
SERENA-2 and SERENA-6 studies, TEAEs were further restricted to those that occurred prior
to the start of any subsequent cancer therapy.
The overall incidence of TEAEs and SAEs, TEAEs leading to dose modifications (dose
interruption or dose reduction [for camizestrant/AI, as applicable]) or discontinuation of
camizestrant/AI, TEAEs resulting in death, AESIs, and TEAEs designated as ADRs were
summarized descriptively by patient count (n), percentage (%) and EAIR in terms of MedDRA
PT and/or CTCAE grade. The most current version of MedDRA was used for coding the AE
data. Data from studies originally reported in previous versions of MedDRA were up-versioned
for integration purposes. For TEAE severity in SERENA-1, CTCAE Version 4.0 was used,
whereas in the SERENA-6, SERENA-2, China, and Japan Phase I studies, CTCAE Version 5.0
was used. The AEs reported remained with the original CTCAE grading recorded in the
individual studies by the investigator. The difference in CTCAE versions between the studies
was not considered to have an impact on the interpretation of the AE summaries.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s categorization of adverse events.

The FDA agrees that the SERENA-6 protocol provided a schedule for laboratory tests and

The protocol required triplicate ECG at screening and following any clinically significant

starting treatment when feasible but could be performed up until Day 1 of Cycle 2.
Safety Results
Deaths
Data:
In SERENA-6, 20 (12.9%) patients in camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm and 18 (11.6%) patients in
AI + CDK4/6i arm died overall. AEs leading to death were reported in 2 patients (1.3%) in the
camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm and 1 patient (0.6%) in the AI + CDK4/6i arm. In addition to
SERENA-6 patients, only one additional death was reported in the camizestrant + CDK4/6i pool
and the cause of death reported was ‘other’.
The Applicant’s Position:
In SERENA-6, the majority of reported deaths were attributed to progression of disease in both
treatment arms. In the camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm, one patient died from acute respiratory
distress syndrome, not related to study treatment, and the second case was a sudden death
(possibly related to camizestrant) with an unknown cause as the patient died at home. In the AI +
CDK4/6i arm, 1 patient died due to ileus, which was considered possibly related to the AI
according to the investigator’s assessment.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of deaths. At the time of the data
cutoff (November 28, 2024), there were 20 deaths in the camizestrant and CDK4/6i arm and 18
deaths on the AI and CDK4/6i arm. Most of these deaths occurred more than 30 days after
stopping trial treatment.
There were 6 deaths on the camizestrant and CDK4/6i arm and 5 deaths on the AI and CDK4/6i
arm that occurred while on trial treatment or within 30 days of stopping treatment. On the
225
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic

Reference ID: 5864804

camizestrant and CDK4/6i arm, 4 of these deaths were due to breast cancer, one was due to acute
respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) and one due to sudden death.
The death due to ARDS occurred in a 60-year-old patient who received treatment with
camizestrant and abemaciclib. On Day 211 on trial, the patient decided to permanently
discontinue treatment (the reason is not clearly documented). On Day 213 the patient presented
to the emergency room due to worsening shortness of breath over the prior 2 days and worsening
productive cough with clear sputum. The patient was hospitalized and admitted to the ICU. The
patient was treated for ARDS with fluids, antibiotics, steroids and pressors. Lab cultures (blood
and sputum) were negative for influenza, RSV, and COVID-19. A chest X-ray and CT scan
performed on Day 213 on trial showed diffuse interstitial thickening throughout both lungs,
concerning for pulmonary edema or interstitial lung disease. The patient developed multiple
organ failure and died on Day 216. It cannot be ruled out that the death was due to trial
treatment. ILD/pneumonitis is included as a Warning in abemaciclib labeling.
All the deaths that occurred while on treatment or within 30 days of stopping treatment on the AI
arm were due to breast cancer. In addition, there was one death on the AI and CDK4/6i arm, due
to ileus, which was considered possibly related to the AI according to the investigator’s
assessment. This death occurred more than 30 days after the last dose of trial treatment.
Serious Adverse Events
Data:
In SERENA-6, SAEs were reported in 16 (10.3%) patients in the camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm
and 19 (12.3%) patients in the AI + CDK4/6i arm. SAEs reported in more than one patient were
osteonecrosis of the jaw, urinary tract infection (in camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm), and anemia (in
AI + CDK4/6i arm), each occurring in 2 patients, while all other SAEs were singular incidents,
reported in only one patient each.
All SAEs were reported in ≤ 2 patients in either pooled safety groups, the only exception was
pyrexia, which was reported in 3 (1.1%) patients in the camizestrant + CDK4/6i pool.
The Applicant’s Position:
In SERENA-6, there were no trends in SAEs in either treatment arm. The incidence of SAEs in
the SERENA-6 camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm was low and comparable to the
camizestrant + CDK4/6i pool. Irrespective of the type of CDK4/6i, no specific trends in SAEs by
PTs were observed in the SERENA-6 and corresponding CDK4/6i pooled data.

The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of SAEs that occurred in SERENA-6 and in

GT included pneumonia and pneumonia respiratory syncytial viral.

The Applicant’s Position:
The low and similar incidence of AEs leading to discontinuation of camizestrant across the
SERENA‑6 camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm, camizestrant +CDK4/6i pool and the monotherapy
pool, reinforces the tolerability of camizestrant whether used alone or in combination with a
CDK4/6i.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees that the discontinuation rate for camizestrant was low on SERENA-6 and agrees
with the adverse events leading to discontinuation as listed in Table 27.
