The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees that dosage modifications, including dose reduction and dosage interruption, of
both camizestrant (or AI) and CDK4/6i occurred more frequently in the camizestrant and
CDK4/6i treatment arm compared to the AI and CDK4/6i treatment arm in SERENA-6. Dose
reductions of camizestrant that occurred in >1% of patients included bradycardia (2.6%) and
electrocardiogram QT prolonged (1.9%). Dosage interruptions of camizestrant that occurred in >
2% of patients included bradycardia (3.9%) and visual disturbances (2.6%).
Although dose reductions of camizestrant were allowed on the SERENA-6 trial, the Applicant is
no longer including dose reductions to manage camizestrant toxicities in ongoing trials. Based
on an Information Request response dated February 27, 2026, the Applicant stated they consider
interruption of camizestrant or permanent discontinuation to be adequate risk minimization
measures based on surveillance of over 5,000 patients dosed with camizestrant in the clinical
development program.
Significant Adverse Events
Data and The Applicant’s Position:
No AEs were classified as other significant AEs in this study.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA disagrees with the Applicant’s position. The Applicant identified bradycardia and
visual disturbances as AEs of special interest (AESIs). The FDA identified QT prolongation as
an additional important AE, particularly in combination with bradycardia as they both increase
the risk of life-threatening arrhythmia. Visual disturbances (GT), bradycardia, and QTc interval
prolongation are discussed further in Section 8.2.5 (Analysis of Submission Specific Safety
Issues).
For further details, refer to the Joint QT and Bradycardia Risk Review dated January 15, 2026.

EAIR is defined as the number of patients with AEs divided by the total number of days at risk for AEs across
all patients in given group, multiplied by 365.25 multiplied by 100.

b

The Applicant’s Position:
The most commonly reported AEs in the SERENA-6 study were consistent with camizestrant
clinical experience to date; and with the known safety profiles of each CDK4/6i (palbociclib,
ribociclib, and abemaciclib).
•
•

•

AEs: The most common AEs (≥ 20% of patients) were hematological toxicities, such as
neutropenia and decreased neutrophil count, and photopsia.
Hematological toxicities were reported more frequently in the camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm
compared to the AI + CDK4/6i arm, which can be explained by the longer duration of
exposure in the camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm.
• When adjusted for duration of exposure, the incidence rates of hematological toxicities
were comparable between the 2 treatment arms, whereas AEs such as headache, nausea,
arthralgia, diarrhea, fatigue, and back pain were noted to be more frequent in the AI +
CDK4/6i arm.
Grade ≥ 3 AEs: These were more frequent in the camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm (60.0%)
versus the AI + CDK4/6i arm (45.8%), though comparable when adjusted for duration of
exposure (EAIR: 117.3 vs 110.9). Frequent Grade ≥ 3 AEs included neutropenia (23.2% vs
16.8%; EAIR: 31.3 vs 30.0) and decreased neutrophil count (22.6% vs 17.4%; EAIR: 28.6 vs
32.4).

In the camizestrant +CDK4/6i pool, the most commonly reported AEs (in ≥ 20% of patients)
included neutropenia, neutrophil count decreased, photopsia, nausea, and diarrhea. The incidence
of hematological AEs (such as neutropenia, neutrophil count decreased, white blood cell count
decreased, and anemia), gastrointestinal AEs (including nausea, diarrhea, and vomiting), as well
as fatigue and headache in the camizestrant with palbociclib, ribociclib, and abemaciclib pools
were comparable to the known safety profiles of each CDK4/6i according to their respective
labels. This consistency indicates that camizestrant does not significantly alter the AE profile of
these CDK4/6i.
In the monotherapy pool, the most commonly reported AEs (in ≥ 10% of patients) were anemia,
sinus bradycardia, photopsia, nausea, diarrhea, and visual impairment. The incidence of these
232
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA

Reference ID: 5864804

common AEs was generally comparable to the camizestrant + CDK4/6i pool. This similarity
indicates that no additional toxicity was observed when camizestrant is combined with a
CDK4/6i.
The safety profile of camizestrant in combination with a CDK4/6i was well characterized in
SERENA-6 and is consistent with the safety profiles of camizestrant and CDK4/6i. No new
safety signals were reported. There are no signs or trends of clinically significant overlapping
toxicity or additive toxicities between camizestrant and the CDK4/6i with which it is combined.
The FDA’s Assessment:
In general, FDA agrees with the presented safety profile for most common adverse events.
However, the FDA does not typically conduct analyses for exposure-adjusted incidence rate
(EAIR). Although the incidence of certain AEs may be higher on the camizestrant and CDK4/6i
arm due to longer treatment exposure, ultimately, this cumulative toxicity is what is experienced
by patients and should be considered in totality without diminishing it based on exposure.
Additionally, there are some differences in the FDA’s analysis compared to the Applicant’s
analysis due to differences in grouping of preferred terms (PTs). In FDA’s analysis, the most
common treatment emergent adverse events (>10%) on the camizestrant and CDK4/6i arm
include neutropenia (55%), musculoskeletal pain (GT; 35%) visual disturbance (GT; 34%),
fatigue (GT; 23%), anemia (17%), dry eye (12%) and nausea (10%). The above listed GT
include the following preferred terms: Musculoskeletal pain (GT): Arthralgia, Arthritis, Back
pain, Bone pain, Musculoskeletal chest pain, Musculoskeletal pain, Myalgia, Neck pain, Noncardiac chest pain, Pain in extremity, and Spinal pain; Visual impairment (GT): Diplopia,
Photophobia, Photopsia, Vision blurred, Visual Field defect, Visual acuity decreased Visual
impairment and visual perseveration; Fatigue (GT): Asthenia, and Fatigue. Visual disturbance
(GT) is discussed further in Section 8.2.5.1.
The FDA disagrees that there were no signs or trends of clinically significant overlapping
toxicity or additive toxicities between camizestrant and the CDK4/6i with which it is combined.
In SERENA-6, only a limited number of patients in the camizestrant and CDK4/6i arm were
treated with camizestrant in combination with either ribociclib (23 patients or 15%) or
abemaciclib (15 patients or 10%). Therefore, although the safety profile for each of these
combinations appears similar to the known safety profile for each individual CDK4/6i, these
combinations were not extensively evaluated in SERENA-6. In addition, there is concern for an
additive and overlapping risk of cardiotoxicity when camizestrant is administered in combination
with ribociclib, as further discussed further under Section 8.2.5.

Laboratory Findings
Data:
•

•

•
•
•

In SERENA-6, no unexpected changes from baseline or trends in hematology values were
observed over time across the treatment arms. Laboratory abnormality thresholds of CTCAE
Grade 3 or 4 were comparable across treatment arms consistent with the known profile of
CDK4/6i. Despite the increased incidence of CTCAE Grade ≥ 3 hematological AEs in the
camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm, the incidence of CTCAE Grade ≥ 3 laboratory values
(hemoglobin, lymphocytes, neutrophils, and platelets) was comparable between treatment
arms, with dose modifications of CDK4/6i due to hematological AEs, which were balanced
across both treatment arms.
The incidence of clinically important hematological abnormalities (hemoglobin, leukocytes,
lymphocytes, neutrophils, and platelets) in the SERENA-6 camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm was
comparable to the camizestrant + CDK4/6i pool, with no specific trends in individual
CDK4/6i groups. These hematological abnormalities were less frequent in the monotherapy
pool compared to SERENA-6 and the camizestrant + CDK4/6i pool, indicating that these are
predominantly driven by CDK4/6i.
In SERENA-6, no unexpected changes or trends from baseline in clinical chemistry values
were observed over time across the different treatment arms.
Additionally, pooled data analysis revealed no evidence of hepatotoxicity in the camizestrant
+ CDK4/6i pool or the monotherapy pool.
No cases of Hy’s Law were reported in SERENA-6.

The Applicant’s Position:
Overall, laboratory parameters were consistent with the safety of camizestrant observed during
the clinical development program and the known safety profile of CDK4/6i per their labels.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment.

No unexpected or clinically meaningful trends or changes from baseline in vital signs or physical
examination safety parameters were observed in SERENA-6 and other supportive studies.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA disagrees that there were no clinically meaningful changes from baseline in vital signs.
In the SERENA-6 trial, baseline heart rate (HR) was similar between the camizestrant and
CDK4/6i arm and the AI and CDK4/6i arm. During the trial, there was a median HR decrease
from baseline of 14 beats/min in the camizestrant and CDK4/6i arm, compared to no change in
the AI and CDK4/6i arm. Bradycardia is discussed further is Section 8.2.5.1.
Electrocardiograms (ECGs)
Data and The Applicant’s Position:
Data were acquired and centrally analyzed through a vendor supplying 12-lead ECG services.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees that the SERENA-6 protocol required triplicate ECG at screening and following
any clinically significant abnormalities. Please reference Section 8.2.5.3 for FDA’s assessment
on QT interval prolongation.
QT
Data and The Applicant’s Position:
Small mean increases in QTcF interval (QT corrected for heart rate) were observed in the
camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm, although no clinically relevant QTcF prolongation was observed.
QTcF duration was sustained throughout treatment. There were 2 patients with an increase from
baseline > 60 ms during the study, both in the camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm.
In SERENA-1 study, QTcI analysis indicated that an effect on ΔQTcI exceeding 10 ms can be
excluded (ie, the upper bound of the 90% CI is below 10 ms) at camizestrant plasma
concentrations up to ~207 ng/mL (~436 ng/mL if the threshold for QTcI was 20 ms). Following
a dose of camizestrant 75 mg QD monotherapy or in combination with palbociclib, ribociclib
(400 and 600 mg) and abemaciclib, the observed steady state camizestrant geomean Cmax values
are all below 207 ng/mL (Table 30).

Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues
Across the camizestrant clinical development program the AESIs included bradycardia (grouped
term including bradycardia and sinus bradycardia; see Section 8.2.5.1) and visual effects
(photopsia, vision blurred, visual impairment, diplopia, photophobia, visual perseveration, and
glare; see Section 8.2.5.2).
During the study period, the AESIs (bradycardia and visual effects) were collected for
camizestrant to better understand and characterize the nature of camizestrant-associated
bradycardia and visual effect events and their incidence, severity, and potential clinical impact
on patients, with particular focus on symptomatic events (ie, for bradycardia, Grade 2 and above)
to provide optimal data for analysis of these AESIs.
8.2.5.1.

The Applicant’s Position:
The vast majority (~90% of events) in the camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm were Grade 1 and none
were SAEs. No treatment discontinuations were due to visual effects, and ophthalmological
review of patients reporting visual effects showed no evidence of structural effects on the eye or
clinically meaningful changes in visual acuity attributed to camizestrant.
Comprehensive ophthalmic evaluations, including BCVA at distance and near, slit lamp
biomicroscopy, pupillary light reflex, extraocular movements, intraocular pressure, confrontation
visual fields, dilated fundus exam, and OCT were performed at baseline, as clinically indicated,
and post-treatment. Across all assessments, >90% of eyes in both arms remained normal or
showed only non-clinically significant changes. Clinically significant findings were rare (< 6%

Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA

per modality), unilateral, non-progressive, and occurred at similar frequencies in both the
treatment arms. No patient experienced bilateral or progressive ≥ 3-line BCVA distance loss,
drug-related visual field defects, or clinically significant ocular hypertension. Structural
evaluations (fundoscopy, OCT) demonstrated preserved retinal and optic nerve integrity, with
isolated abnormalities unaccompanied by functional change. No ophthalmic event led to dose
reduction, treatment discontinuation or SAE reporting.
Overall incidence of visual effects in the SERENA-6 camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm was
comparable to the camizestrant + CDK4/6i pool and the monotherapy pool, with 39.9% and
29.1% experiencing visual effects in the combination pool and monotherapy pool, respectively.
The events were predominantly Grade 1 (36.9% and 28.2%, respectively), while CTCAE
Grade 2 AEs were experienced by 2.7% and 0.9% of patients, respectively. Photopsia (23.2%
and 13.7%, respectively) was the most commonly reported PT. Dose interruptions due to visual
effects occurred in 4 patients (1.5%) in the combination pool; no dose reduction or
discontinuation was reported due to visual effects in the pooled datasets. Most of the visual
effects were resolved by the safety follow-up in all the treatment groups.
Taken together, these results demonstrate preserved ocular structure and function and indicate
that camizestrant was not associated with any treatment-related ocular safety signal.
The FDA’s Assessment:
In general, the FDA agrees with Applicant’s position. However, the FDA does not typically
review adverse events based on attribution as described by the Applicant in Table 31 (foot note
c), as attribution can be subjective and prone to bias.
The FDA prefers use of visual disturbances as the GT rather than visual effects since it better
characterizes the individual preferred terms (PT). For visual disturbances, in addition to the
Applicant’s grouping, the FDA included the PTs visual field defect (1.9%) and visual acuity
decreased (1.3%) in the visual disturbances GT, resulting in 34% of patients in the camizestrant
and CDK4/6i arm compared to 16% in the AI and CDK4/6i arm experiencing visual disturbances
of any grade. The FDA agrees that the most common PT under visual disturbances was
photopsia (20% in the camizestrant and CDK4/6i arm and 8% in the AI and CDK4/6i arm). All
cases of visual disturbances (GT) were Grade 1 and 2, except for one case of Grade 3 photopsia.
The visual PROs are further discussed in Section 8.2.6.
For further details, refer to the Ophthalmology Consultation by Drs. Jennifer Hammer and Rhea
Lloyd dated November 19, 2025.

The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

Source: Table 2.7.4.5.1b; ISS Module 5.3.5.3.

The Applicant’s Position:
In SERENA-6, bradycardia events were mostly asymptomatic and exhibited an early,
time-dependent onset, with a gradual decrease in heart rate over a period of approximately
14 days, reaching a stable nadir at that time. Importantly, there were no Grade ≥ 3 bradycardia
events or patient discontinuations due to bradycardia. No cases of TdP, ventricular tachycardia,
ventricular fibrillation or flutter were reported.
The trend and incidence for bradycardia was consistent across the SERENA-6 study,
camizestrant + CDK4/6i combination pool and the camizestrant monotherapy pool; the incidence
of CTCAE Grade 2 was low and comparable with SERENA-6, there were no CTCAE Grade ≥ 3
bradycardia events, and no patient discontinued camizestrant due to AEs of bradycardia in the
camizestrant + CDK4/6i pool or in the monotherapy pool.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA disagrees with elements of the Applicant’s position. The FDA does not typically
review adverse events based on attribution, as described by the Applicant in Table 31 (foot note
c), as attribution can be subjective and prone to bias. Furthermore, while there were no cases of
bradycardia associated with severe AEs (e.g., syncope) in SERENA-6, the heart rate lowering
effect (14 bpm median decrease) with use of camizestrant may increase the risk of clinically
significant bradycardia, particularly for patients with low baseline heart rate. Bradycardia is a
risk factor for development of Torsades de Pointes (TdP). This is particularly concerning because
camizestrant may also have a QTc interval-prolonging effect and may be administered
concomitantly with other QTc interval-prolonging drugs such as ribociclib, increasing cardiac
risk. Of note, although there were no cases of bradycardia associated with syncope in SERENA6, there was one case of syncope for a patient on SERENA-1 who developed bradycardia and QT
prolongation, resulting in TdP, which is described below in the next section.
For further details, refer to the Joint QT and Bradycardia Risk Review dated January 15, 2026.
8.2.5.3.

QT Prolongation and Risk of Life-Threatening Arrhythmia

The FDA disagrees with the Applicant’s assessment of QTc interval prolongation presented in
their portion of Section 8.2.4. Additionally, the FDA is concerned about the proarrhythmic
243
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

potential of camizestrant arising from the combination of bradycardia and QTc interval
prolongation, especially when camizestrant is combined with other drugs that have QTc interval
prolonging effects and/or that may increase camizestrant exposure. The FDA’s assessment is
based on the following considerations.
•
•

•

•

•

•

Nonclinical assays and evaluations in dogs demonstrated camizestrant's concentrationdependent potential for QT interval prolongation.
In the phase 1 trial SERENA-1, the Applicant demonstrated concentration-dependent QT
interval prolongation using both QTcI (individually corrected QT) and QTcF (Fridericiacorrected QT) analyses over the camizestrant dose range of 75 mg to 300 mg QD.
Notably, the Applicant's exposure-response analyses gave conflicting results depending
on the QTc correction method employed: the estimated increase in the QTc interval from
baseline at mean steady-state Cmax for camizestrant 75 mg QD monotherapy (proposed
dose) was 3.0 msec for QTcI and 16 msec for QTcF.
FDA identified concerns with reliance on the individual QTc interval correction
methodology (QTcI) when used in the setting of a drug that causes large reductions in
heart rate. Therefore, FDA believes these data are insufficient to characterize the QTc
interval prolongation effect, and that the QTcI method may underestimate the true QTc
interval effect of camizestrant.
FDA considers that any increase in QTc interval warrants careful evaluation, particularly
in the presence of concomitant bradycardia as is the case with camizestrant. Bradycardia
represents a well-established risk factor for TdP. The combination of QTc interval
prolongation and bradycardia synergistically increases the risk of drug-induced TdP, as
bradycardia prolongs ventricular repolarization and increases the likelihood of early
afterdepolarizations that can trigger ventricular arrhythmias.
FDA is particularly concerned about coadministration of camizestrant with ribociclib,
due to both pharmacokinetic (PK) and pharmacodynamic (PD) interactions that increase
the risk of QTc interval prolongation and TdP. From a PK perspective, co-administration
of camizestrant 75 mg QD with ribociclib 600 mg QD (dosing regimen in SERENA-6)
increases camizestrant exposure; camizestrant steady-state exposure was approximately
2-fold higher for the combination compared with camizestrant 75 mg monotherapy. From
a PD perspective, the combination creates additive QTc interval prolongation effects as
ribociclib itself causes concentration-dependent increases in QTcF with a mean increase
of around 20 msec (Ribociclib USPI).
In SERENA-1, two cohorts investigated camizestrant 75 mg QD co-administered with
ribociclib at two dose levels (400 mg or 600 mg QD). Investigators reported TEAEs of
ECG QT prolongation in 6 out of 28 patients (21%) receiving camizestrant 75 mg +
244
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)

In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

•

ribociclib 400 mg and 5 out of 32 patients (16%) receiving camizestrant 75 mg +
ribociclib 600 mg. Two patients had a reported QTcF >500 msec with change from
baseline >60 msec.
Most critically, one patient receiving camizestrant 75 mg + ribociclib 600 mg
experienced Grade 3 syncope followed by ventricular fibrillation (Grade 4) and a serious
adverse event of ECG QT prolongation (Grade 4) one week after a reported QTcF >500
msec. FDA reviewed the ECG waveforms for this patient and confirmed significant QTc
interval prolongation, bradycardia, and ultimately TdP. Although there were confounding
factors in this case, the FDA cannot exclude the contributions of camizestrant and
ribociclib to this event.

In summary, camizestrant has dose dependent heart rate lowering and potentially has QTc
itnerval prolonging effects (insuffient information to fully characterize the effect size at the time
of this review), both of which synergistically increase proarrhythmic risk. Coadministration of
camizestrant with ribociclib, itself a known QTc interval prolonging drug which can also
increase the exposure of camizestrant, further increases the risk of bradycardia and QTc interval
prolonging potential. Furthermore, one patient in SERENA-1 who received camizestrant and
ribociclib at the doses used in SERENA-6 experienced QTc interval prolongation and lifethreatening arrhythmia (TdP). Only 23 patients enrolled to SERENA-6 received camizestrant and
ribociclib, limiting further evaluation of the cardiac safety for this combination.
Clinical Outcome Assessment (COA) Analyses Informing Safety/Tolerability
Data and The Applicant’s Position:
PRO-CTCAE: Results from exploratory endpoints as measured by the PRO-CTCAE (decreased
appetite, nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, arm or leg swelling, heart palpitations, hair loss, dizziness,
blurred vision, headache, aching joints, fatigue, and hot flashes) over time were assessed. In the
patients’ worst PRO-CTCAE response within 12 weeks, there were no noticeable differences
between treatment arms.
PGI-TT: Overall patient-reported treatment tolerability as measured by the PGI-TT (ie, “in the
last 7 days, how bothered were you by the side effects of your cancer treatment?”) was generally
comparable between the camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm and the AI + CDK4/6i arm. Most patients
reported either "none" or "a little bit" of bother from their side effects (other potential responses
were: "somewhat," "quite a bit," or "very much"). Specifically, in both arms, > 65% of patients
reported either "none" or "a little bit" of bother from their side effects across all time points with
245
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

≥ 10 patients per arm, while proportions of patients reporting being "somewhat," "quite a bit," or
"very much" bothered were low and comparable between arms.
VSAQ: On the VSAQ, At baseline, 16% of patients reported visual effects in both arms. If
experienced, treatment-emergent visual effects appeared early, with both arms reporting an
increased number of patients with effects at 2 weeks (more in the camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm
than in the AI plus CDK4/6i arm), which then remained stable. Patients typically reported visual
effects occurring ≤ 3 times per week, of short duration (< 1 minute) and with no or a low degree
of bother. Median impact on activities of daily living was ‘minimal’ with no evidence of
worsening over time. Reversibility was evident with only < 7% of patients in both arms reporting
visual effects 4 weeks post treatment.
NEI-VFQ-25: The NEI-VFQ-25 was administered at baseline, every 6 months, and at treatment
discontinuation. Values for the composite score and all subscales (ie, global vision, vision
specific social functioning, vision specific mental health, vision specific role difficulties, vision
specific dependency, difficulty with difficulty, difficulty with color vision, and peripheral vision)
were comparable between the two treatment arms and remained stable over time.
The FDA’s Assessment:
In general, the patient-reported symptoms describing treatment side effects support the clinician
observed AEs. No formal comparison should be made between arms, and this information was
considered descriptive.
FDA sent a series of information requests to the Applicant on August 19–20, 2026, seeking
clarification on the Applicant-reported completion rates and descriptive analysis results for PROCTCAE Blurred Vision (Severity), VSAQ, and PGI-TT. In response, the Applicant provided the
source documents from which the numbers were derived, along with the code and datasets for
reproducibility. Ultimately, FDA was able to reconcile the results presented in the label with the
source documentation provided by the Applicant.

Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups
The Applicant’s Position:
The camizestrant safety profile has been assessed in the SERENA-6 camizestrant + CDK4/6i
arm and the camizestrant + CDK4/6i pool in relation to the following intrinsic factors: age, race
(White, Asian, and Other), ethnicity (Hispanic or Latino and Non-Hispanic or Latino), disease

site (liver visceral, non-liver visceral and non-visceral), prior chemotherapy in the advanced

The FDA agrees that the safety profile for camizestrant and CDK4/6i in patients aged 65-74
years compared to those aged <65 years generally appeared similar. There were only 22 patients
aged 75 and older on the SERENA-6 trial, making it difficult to draw any conclusions from this
small subgroup. Similarly, there were few patients of “other or Black/African American” race,
Hispanic/Latino ethnicity and male sex, making it difficult to comment on these subgroups.

There are no data from the use of camizestrant in pregnant women.

Female patients of childbearing potential enrolled in camizestrant studies must have had a
negative pregnancy test prior to the start of dosing and use highly effective contraception
throughout the study and 4 weeks after dosing. Male patients with female partners of
reproductive potential must be surgically sterile or use highly effective contraception throughout
the study and 1 week after dosing.

The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position.

Camizestrant has not been studied in pediatric patients.

The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position.

Reference ID: 5864804

The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA disagrees that the safety of camizestrant has been adequately characterized in
combination with the three different CDK 4/6I in SERENA-6. Camizestrant is associated with a
heart rate lowering effect and QT prolonging effects. When camizestrant is co-administered with
other drugs that have QT prolonging effects, such as ribociclib, there is an increased risk of
developing a life-threatening arrhythmia. Ribociclib also increases the exposure of camizestrant,
which could further potentiate camizestrant’s heart rate lowering and QT prolonging effects.
Only 23 patients enrolled in SERENA-6 received camizestrant and ribociclib.
However, the FDA review team is recommending accelerated approval with post-marketing
requirements to further characterize the cardiac safety of camizestrant, particularly in
combination with ribociclib.
Integrated Assessment of Safety
Data:
All relevant safety data are described in Sections 8.2.1 to 8.2.10.
The Applicant’s Position:
The most common adverse reactions (in ≥ 20% of patients) were visual effects (31.6%),
neutropenia (29.0%), and neutrophil count decreased (27.1%).
The incidence of AEs leading to discontinuation of camizestrant/AI by all causality were very
low and balanced across treatment arms (1.3% in the camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm and 1.9% in
the AI + CDK4/6i arm), suggesting acceptable long-term tolerability. The incidence of AEs
leading to CDK4/6i discontinuation by all causality were also very low and balanced across
treatment arms (1.3% in each arm). Only 1 patient in the camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm compared
with 3 patients in the AI + CDK4/6i arm had an AE leading to discontinuation of
camizestrant/AI that was considered possibly related to camizestrant/AI by the investigator.
There was an increase in reported AEs of visual effects in the camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm
compared with reporting in the AI + CDK4/6i arm. However, of the visual effects associated
with camizestrant treatment, the vast majority (> 90%) were reported as Grade 1, generally
occurred within 2 weeks of commencing treatment, occurred in morning/evening, and were of
short duration with minimal to low impact on daily activities (level of difficulty with driving,
difficulty with role, and difficulty with color vision). The vast majority of patients “were not at
250

Reference ID: 5864804

all bothered” or only “a little bothered” by these effects. Comprehensive ophthalmologic
assessments demonstrated no treatment-related structural or functional ocular safety signals
associated with camizestrant. Across both eyes and all assessment methods, the camizestrant +
CDK4/6i arm was not associated with any clinically meaningful decline in visual acuity under
best-corrected conditions.
Camizestrant-related bradycardia (grouped term) was observed (7.7% of patients in the
camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm) but treatment modifications due to bradycardia were low.
No treatment discontinuations due to bradycardia were reported. There were no Grade ≥ 3 events
reported. In the camizestrant + CDK4/6i arm there was a decrease of 14 bpm in median heart rate
on Day 15 (from 73 bpm median at baseline), reaching a stable nadir at that time. Decreases in
median heart rate by ECG had recovered to baseline at the safety follow-up.
The safety profile of camizestrant in combination with a CDK4/6i (palbociclib, ribociclib, or
abemaciclib) in the SERENA-6 study is consistent with the camizestrant clinical experience to
date and with the known safety profiles of each individual CDK4/6i. There are no signs or trends
of clinically significant overlapping toxicity or additive toxicities between camizestrant and the
CDK4/6i with which it is combined.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Although the safety profile for camizestrant and CDK4/6i was generally as expected for an ET
combined with CDK4/6i, camizestrant was associated with the additional toxicities of
bradycardia, QT prolongation, and visual disturbances. FDA’s safety review focused on better
understanding these additional toxicities.
The FDA disagrees with the Applicant that there are no signs of clinically significant
overlapping or additive toxicities between camizestrant and the CDK4/6i with which is
combined. The FDA is concerned about the potential for camizestrant to cause life-threatening
arrhythmias, particularly when administered with other drugs that have QTc interval prolonging
effects and/or that may increase camizestrant exposure, such as ribociclib. Camizestrant is
associated with a heart rate lowering effect (14 bpm median decrease), which may increase the
risk of clinically significant bradycardia, particularly for patients with a low heart rate at
baseline. Bradycardia is also a risk factor for development of clinically significant arrhythmias,
such as ventricular fibrillation or TdP. While there is insufficient information to fully
characterize the effect size of camizestrant on the QTc interval at the proposed clinical dose,
when bradycardia and QTc interval prolongation are combinedthis synergistically increases
proarrhythmic risk.

The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA

Reference ID: 5864804

When camizestrant is co-administered with other drugs that have QTc interval prolonging
effects, such as ribociclib, this further increases the risk of developing a life-threatening
arrhythmia. Ribociclib also increases the exposure of camizestrant, which could further
potentiate camizestrant’s heart rate lowering and potential QTc interval prolonging effects. One
patient enrolled in the phase 1 trial SERENA-1 received camizestrant and ribociclib and
experienced bradycardia, QT prolongation and life-threatening arrhythmia (TdP). Although there
were confounding factors in this case, the FDA cannot exclude the contributions of camizestrant
and ribociclib to this event. Only 23 patients enrolled to SERENA-6 received camizestrant and
ribociclib, limiting further evaluation of the cardiac safety for this combination. Although the
risk of a life-threatening arrhythmia may be small, patients could be accepting the possibility of
this serious risk to switch treatment for uncertain clinical benefit.
Otherwise, regarding visual disturbances, the FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s
characterization. Visual disturbances were mostly low grade, with the most common TEAE
being photopsia. Photopsia cases were reported as brief periods (<1 min/episode) of flashing
lights that were reversible and not associated with structural findings on ophthalmic examination.
The PRO data discussed in Section 8.2.6 support clinician reported observations that these events
were not severe, of short duration, and had minimal impact on daily living.
The other toxicities observed, including arthralgia, fatigue, and hematologic toxicities such as
neutropenia, were generally as expected for an ET and CDK4/6i. The most common adverse
events that occurred in both treatment arms of SERENA-6 were hematologic toxicities.

SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS
Statistical Issues
The FDA’s Assessment:
No major statistical issues were identified. The primary efficacy endpoint of PFS was met at
DCO1 with a statistically significant improvement in PFS for the camizestrant and CDK4/6i arm
compared to the AI and CDK4/6i arm (HR=0.44 [95% CI: 0.31, 0.60]; p<0.00001). The
estimated median PFS was 16 months (95% CI: 12.7, 18.2) for the investigational arm and 9.2
months (95% CI: 7.2, 9.5) for the control arm, respectively. The PFS results were consistent
across sensitivity and exploratory subgroup analyses.
252
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

The key secondary endpoint of PFS2 was met at DCO3 with a HR of 0.63 (95% CI: 0.46, 0.86)
and a p-value of 0.00373. However, in general, interpretability of PFS2 is undermined by both
the endpoint’s inability to isolate the effect of the investigational drug, and subjectivity and
heterogeneity:
•

•

Subjectivity: The timing and choice of subsequent therapy were at the investigator’s
discretion, and investigators were allowed to determine a PFS2 event by one or more of
the following: clinical progression, unequivocal radiological progression, and RECIST
progression.
Heterogeneity: Regional differences in drug availability and local standards created
heterogeneous subsequent treatment options, potentially resulting in differential efficacy
among patients receiving different subsequent therapies.

At DCO3, the key secondary endpoint of OS was not statistically significant at an interim
analysis, conducted at 58% information fraction, which showed a HR of 0.87 (95% CI: 0.57,
1.30). FDA notes that SERENA-6 was designed with a target power of 63% for OS, and OS may
not demonstrate statistical significance at final analysis. The final analysis of OS is expected to
occur in 2028.
Despite the statistically significant primary efficacy results, uncertainties remain. The primary
endpoint of PFS was measured from detection of an ESR1 mutation rather than radiographic
progression. At PFS, patients had received unequal numbers of new treatment regimens between
arms. Ultimately, SERENA-6 was not designed to assess the clinical benefit of the proposed
experimental strategy of receiving a new treatment (i.e., camizestrant) at detection of an ESR1m
compared to receiving it at radiographic progression for the intended patient population. No
external data exist that would have enabled assessment of the experimental treatment-switch
strategy, much less support it.
For confirmation of clinical benefit, the Applicant agreed to two PMRs. As the first PMR, the
Applicant will conduct a new trial comparing the early treatment-switching strategy to the
standard treatment-switching strategy. In the trial, patients with HR+HER2- MBC with an
ESR1m detected after at least 6 months of treatment with an AI and CDK4/6i will be randomized
to either switch treatment to camizestrant and CDK4/6i or continue with AI and CDK4/6i and
switch to camizestrant with or without CDK4/6i at radiographic progression. The primary
endpoint will be time to strategy failure (TSF), defined as time from randomization to either
progressive disease by RECIST v1.1 while receiving camizestrant with or without CDK4/6i, or
death, whichever occurs first. This trial will not—nor is it intended to—isolate the effect of
camizestrant but rather appraise the treatment strategy.

Reference ID: 5864804

As the second PMR, the Applicant further agreed to furnish FDA with final OS results from
SERENA-6.

Conclusions and Recommendations
The FDA’s Assessment:
SERENA-6 is an ongoing, multiregional, randomized, placebo-controlled trial to evaluate the
efficacy and safety of switching to camizestrant after detecting an ESR1m versus continuing on
an AI and CDK4/6I in patients with ER+/HER2- advanced or metastatic breast cancer. A total of
3,256 patients were tested for ESR1m, and 315 patients were randomized in a ratio of 1:1 to
receive camizestrant with the prior CDK4/6i or to continue on the prior AI and CDK4/6i at
ESR1m detection prior to radiographic progression. Randomization was stratified by timing of
ESR1m detection (first or subsequent test), time on aromatase inhibitor and CDK4/6 inhibitor (<
18 months or ≥ 18 months), and CDK4/6i (palbociclib or ribociclib or abemaciclib). Patients
received trial treatment until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity. Crossover to
camizestrant at progression was not permitted.
The estimated median investigator-assessed PFS at DCO1 was 16.0 months for the camizestrant
and CDK4/6i arm and 9.2 months for the AI and CDK4/6i arm. At DCO1, PFS demonstrated
statistical significance with a HR of 0.44 (95% CI: 0.31, 0.60, p < 0.00001). At DCO3, PFS2
demonstrated statistical significance with a HR of 0.63 (95% CI: 0.46, 0.86, p = 0.00373). The
estimated median PFS2 at DCO3 was 25.7 months for the camizestrant and CDK4/6I arm and
19.1 months for the AI and CDK4/6i arm. At DCO3, OS failed to reach statistical significance at
a HR of 0.87 (95% CI: 0.57, 1.30).
The following are the key issues identified for this application:
•

•

SERENA-6 was not designed to evaluate whether switching treatment at detection of
ESR1m rather than at radiographic progression is beneficial to patients. Furthermore, to
our knowledge, no clinical trials have established that the experimental strategy is
superior. Compounding this design limitation, no crossover to camizestrant and CDK4/6i
was permitted at disease progression for patients on the control arm. Moreover, patients
in SERENA-6 who were randomized to continue AI and CDK4/6i were able to remain on
this therapy for an estimated median of 9.2 months (95% CI: 7.2, 9.5) prior to disease
progression or death. By switching therapy early, patients may not be maximizing each
line of treatment.
Although PFS was measured from randomization, the starting point for randomization,
upon detection of an ESR1m, was new, and the clinical meaningfulness of the PFS
improvement measured from ESR1m detection is uncertain. The FDA has accepted PFS
254
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)

In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

•

•

as an endpoint in HR+HER2- MBC based on the premise that clinical trials are enrolling
patients (and measuring PFS) at times when a new treatment is clearly needed, i.e., after
diagnosis of advanced or metastatic disease (1L trials) or after a patient experiences
radiographic progression on current therapy (2L and later trials). The clinical
meaningfulness of a PFS improvement measured from detection of a resistance mutation
to current therapy with an AI is uncertain, and the magnitude of PFS improvement that
may translate into longer-term benefit for a patient is unknown.
PFS2 is inadequate to provide evidence of clinical benefit. The FDA does not typically
use PFS2 for regulatory decision-making, as it does not isolate the effect of the
experimental drug. Additionally, PFS2 is unreliable because the timing and choice of
next treatment in SERENA-6 were at investigators’ discretion and influenced by factors
such as regional availability and local standards. Patients on the control arm were not
permitted to cross over to camizestrant and CDK4/6i at disease progression, which would
have otherwise allowed some characterization of the effect of an early switch at ESR1m
detection rather than radiographic progression—albeit exploratory. Along with
radiographic progression based on RECIST v1.1, investigators could also consider nonRECIST radiographic progression or clinical progression as a PFS2 progression event,
adding more subjectivity into this endpoint. Furthermore, in SERENA-6, PFS2 also did
not address whether the experimental strategy of receiving the new treatment at detection
of ESR1m is beneficial compared to receiving it at radiographic disease progression
because the trial was not designed to do so. At PFS2, patients on the experimental arm
would have received two new treatments since randomization, and patients on the control
arm would have continued their previous regimen (AI and CDK4/6i) and received one
new treatment regimen. There would be an imbalance in the number of new treatment
regimens patients received on the two arms without an assessment of whether changing
regimens at ESR1m is beneficial compared to changing regimens at radiographic
progression.
At DCO3, OS was immature, and the trial was underpowered for this endpoint. An OS
benefit for camizestrant in SERENA-6 could reduce the uncertainties related to whether
changing therapy at ESR1m detection—rather than at radiographic progression—offers
long-term patient benefit. However, with an information fraction (IF) of 58% at the latest
DCO, the final OS analysis not anticipated until approximately 2028, and a target power
of only 63%, FDA is concerned that SERENA-6 may not reach statistical significance for
OS.

Collectively, these issues complicate the interpretation of the PFS and PFS2 results from
SERENA-6 and limit the extent to which the statistically significant findings can be taken as
evidence of clinical benefit for camizestrant in the intended patient population.

The Applicant asserted the following:
•
•
•
•

The PFS improvement demonstrated in SERENA-6 was evidence of clinical benefit for
camizestrant in patients with a tumor ESR1 mutation, prior to radiographic progression.
The PFS definition in SERENA-6 (measured from randomization to investigator
determined progression by RECIST criteria) was not different than in other trials in early
ER+ ABC.
SERENA-6 was not designed to address the question of whether switching therapy to
camizestrant at detection of an ESR1 mutation is superior to switching at radiographic
progression, and this information is not needed to show clinical benefit.
PFS2, time to chemotherapy/ADC, and PROs supported the observed improvement in
PFS which they considered clinically meaningful. OS information is supportive because
there is no evidence of a potential detriment.

Committee members were asked to vote on the following question:
Based on the results of SERENA-6, has clinically meaningful benefit for camizestrant
been demonstrated for the treatment of patients with HR+/HER2- metastatic breast
cancer with a tumor ESR1 mutation detected while on aromatase inhibitor and CDK4/6
inhibitor treatment, prior to radiographic progression?
The Committee voted 6–3 that SERENA-6 did not demonstrate a clinically meaningful benefit
for camizestrant in the intended patient population.
In their rationale for their vote, most Committee members agreed with the FDA that SERENA-6
failed to answer the key question of whether switching therapy at detection of an ESR1m, rather
than at radiographic progression, provides a clinically meaningful benefit. One Committee
member stated that, because this strategy is paradigm changing, the evidentiary standard should
be high given the far-reaching implications an endorsement of SERENA-6’s trial design would
have for future trials. Several Committee members were concerned that if camizestrant were
approved based on the results of SERENA-6, patients and clinicians could be given false hope
that earlier treatment provides a clinical benefit. They agreed with FDA that a PFS improvement
that is measured from randomization triggered by ESR1m detection is difficult to interpret. They
also stated that even if PFS were sufficient to demonstrate clinical benefit, based on the
SERENA-6 results, relatively few patients may have actually benefited compared to the large
number who were screened. Finally, Committee members who voted in the negative stated that

The FDA’s Assessment:
The Applicant's E-R safety analysis is acceptable. We note that both bradycardia and visual
effects demonstrate significant positive E-R relationships in monotherapy (both p<0.001),
supporting dose-dependent toxicity. The monotherapy E-R analysis demonstrates a statistically
significant and clinically meaningful relationship between camizestrant Cmaxss and bradycardia
incidence (p<0.001, N=292). The model shows bradycardia probability increases from
approximately 23% at 25 mg QD to 64% at 450 mg QD, supporting dose-dependent cardiac
toxicity. However, the conclusion should be interpreted with caution when extrapolating to
combination therapy, as the SERENA-6 data included only one camizestrant dose level (75 mg
QD).

19.4.3.6.

Overall benefit-risk evaluation based on E-R analyses

The Applicant’s Position:
In summary, no exposure-response relationship was identified for patients in SERENA-6,
suggesting that a 75 mg QD dose of camizestrant in combination with a CDK4/6i has acceptable
benefit-to-risk profile in the targeted patient population.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant in general. See section 8 for comments on benefit-risk profile.
Physiologically based Pharmacokinetic Modeling Analyses
19.4.4.1.

Executive Summary

The FDA’s Assessment:
The objective of this review is to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant’s physiologically based
pharmacokinetic (PBPK) analyses to:
•
•
•
•
•
•

evaluate the drug-drug interaction (DDI) potential of camizestrant as an object of
moderate and weak CYP3A inhibitors;
evaluate the DDI potential of camizestrant as an object of strong, moderate, and weak
CYP3A inducers;
evaluate the DDI potential of camizestrant as an object of moderate CYP3A inducers
when co-administered with ribociclib;
evaluate the DDI potential of camizestrant as an inhibitor of CYP2B6, CYP2C9,
CYP2C19, and CYP2D6;
evaluate the DDI potential of camizestrant as an inhibitor of P-gp, OATP1Bs, and BCRP;
and
evaluate the impact of mild, moderate, and severe hepatic impairment on the steady-state
camizestrant exposure in the presence or absence of ribociclib.

The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the PBPK report (D8534C00001) and related
model summary reports, the responses to FDA PBPK information requests (IR) dated 08 August
2025 (seq0019), 01 December 2025 (seq0037), and 22 January 2026 (seq0043), and the
modeling support files, and concluded that:
•

No dose adjustment is needed when co-administrated with a moderate or weak CYP3A
inhibitor, given that the moderate CYP3A inhibitors fluconazole, erythromycin, and
verapamil were predicted to increase camizestrant exposure by approximately 1.6fold following once-daily administration of 75 mg camizestrant.

•

Camizestrant should not be used with strong CYP3A inducers, given that the strong
CYP3A inducers rifampin and carbamazepine were predicted to
reduce camizestrant exposure by approximately 80%.

•

The dose of camizestrant should be doubled (i.e., to 150 mg QD) in patients who are
receiving camizestrant in combination with abemaciclib or palbociclib if moderate
CYP3A inducers cannot be avoided, given that the moderate CYP3A inducers
phenobarbital and efavirenz were predicted to reduce camizestrant exposure by
approximately 60%.

•

No dose adjustment is needed in patients who are receiving camizestrant in combination
with ribociclib when a moderate CYP3A inducer is coadministered given
that camizestrant exposure is not expected to be reduced.

•

No dose adjustment is needed when co-administrated with a weak CYP3A inducer, given
that the weak CYP3A inducer modafinil was predicted to reduce camizestrant exposure
by approximately 20%.

•

Concomitant use of camizestrant with CYP2C9 or CYP2C19 substrates should be
avoided given that camizestrant is predicted to be a strong inhibitor of CYP2C9 and
CYP2C19

•

The potential interactions of camizestrant with substrates of P-gp and/or BCRP cannot be
ruled out, and the in-vitro DDI liability should be retained in the label.

•

The effects of hepatic impairment on steady-state camizestrant exposure may be
comparable to those observed following a single dose of camizestrant when camizestrant
is administered alone.

•

When camizestrant is co-administered with ribociclib in patients with severe hepatic
impairment, the dosing frequency of camizestrant is recommended to reduce to every
other day (i.e., reduce the dosage to 75 mg QOD).

Reference ID: 5864804

19.4.4.2.

Background

Camizestrant (also known as AZD9833; formerly known as AZ14066724) is an oral selective
estrogen receptor degrader (SERD) and estrogen receptor (ER) antagonist in development for the
treatment of adult patients with ER-positive, human epidermal growth factor receptor 2 (HER2)negative, locally advanced or metastatic breast cancer upon emergence of an ESR1 mutation
during first-line endocrine-based therapy, in combination with a CDK4/6 inhibitor (palbociclib,
ribociclib, or abemaciclib). The proposed recommended dosage of camizestrant is 75 mg PO QD
with or without food. The only proposed commercial dose strength is a 75 mg tablet.
Following oral administration of single and multiple once-daily doses of camizestrant in patients
with metastatic breast cancer, camizestrant was steadily absorbed, and the time to peak
concentration was achieved approximately 2 to 4 hours post-dose (D8530C00001). Camizestrant
has nonlinear pharmacokinetics. Camizestrant AUC and Cmax increased greater than doseproportionally over the dose range from 25 to 450 mg (D8530C00001). Steady state was
achieved by Day 5 following once daily dosing with approximately 1.8-fold accumulation.
Compared to the fasted state, similar camizestrant exposures were observed when
coadministered with a high-fat meal. The fed-to-fasted geometric mean ratios were
approximately 1.06- and 1.10-fold, respectively (D8530C00004). The apparent oral clearance
(CL/F) of camizestrant in participants with advanced breast cancer (D8530C00001,
D8530C00006, and D8530C00007) was similar to that observed in healthy subjects
(D8530C00004) following a single dose of 75 mg camizestrant.
Camizestrant is primarily metabolized by CYP3A4 and UGT1A4 in vitro (BS001884-89,
BS005039-01, and 02). Following coadministration of 200 mg itraconazole (capsule) once daily
for 6 days with a single dose of 75 mg camizestrant on Day 4 in healthy subjects, camizestrant
AUC and Cmax increased by 1.9- and 1.4-fold, respectively (D8532C00003). The results from
the human ADME study in healthy postmenopausal female subjects (D8532C00005), conducted
with a single oral dose of 75 mg camizestrant, showed that approximately 82% of the
radioactivity administered was recovered, of which approximately 16.8% and 65.1% of the
radioactivity were recovered in the urine and feces, respectively. Approximately 14% and 4.8%
of the dose were excreted in feces and urine as unchanged drug, respectively. The absolute
bioavailability study, conducted with a single oral dose of 75 or 300 mg camizestrant (late-phase
tablet) with an intravenous (IV) microdose of camizestrant (100 g), showed that camizestrant
had an oral bioavailability of 43% and 55%, and total plasma clearance of 51.4 and 52.6 L/h,
respectively (D8530C00004). Following oral administration of a single 75 mg dose of
camizestrant, the oral clearance was 69 L/h and a renal clearance was 4 L/h (D8532C00005).
309
Version date: March 1, 2024 (All NDA/BLA reviews)
In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant,
which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

Based on in vitro drug interaction studies, camizestrant is a reversible inhibitor of CYP3A,
CYP2B6, and CYP2D6 (BS001884-92 2018 and BS001884-90 2018) and a time-dependent
inhibitor of CYP2C9 and CYP2C19 (BS001884-40 2017). In addition, camizestrant inhibits
CES2 enzyme in vitro with a Ki of 19.1 μM (BE003623-73 2022). A midazolam DDI study was
conducted in healthy subjects following coadministration of once-daily doses of 75 mg
camizestrant tablet for 6 days with a single dose of 1 mg midazolam oral syrup on Day 6
(D8532C00006). Midazolam AUC and Cmax were increased 2- and 1.5-fold, respectively. In
vitro, camizestrant is not an inducer of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A (CYP0750 R108).
Camizestrant is a substrate of P-gp (CYP0750_R99), but not a substrate of BCRP, OATP1B1,
OATP1B3 (CYP0750_R99). Camizestrant is an inhibitor of P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, OCT2,
MATE1, and MATE2-K (BS001884-86, -87, and -88, and CYP0750_R71). Refer to the Clinical
Pharmacology review section for detailed information on camizestrant regarding ADME
properties and in vitro and clinical studies used in PBPK modeling.
19.4.4.3.

Methods

All simulations were performed using the PK/PD Profiles mode in the Simcyp® Simulator
(Versions 22 and 24, Certara, Sheffield, UK). The PBPK modeling and simulation strategy is
illustrated in Figure 19.11, which summarizes the studies used for camizestrant model
development and verification, and model applications in predicting DDIs of camizestrant as an
object of CYP3A4 and UGT1A4 induction or inhibition and as a precipitant of various
transporters and enzymes. The final model input parameters are summarized in Table 19.6. The
camizestrant PBPK model consists of a first-order absorption model, a minimal PBPK model
with a single adjusting compartment for distribution, and an enzyme kinetics model and renal
clearance for elimination. The Simcyp library files for SV-itraconazole, erythromycin,
verapamil, fluconazole, cimetidine, rifampin (SV-Rifampicin-MD), efavirenz, phenobarbital,
carbamazepine, midazolam, alfentanil, alprazolam, rivaroxaban, S-warfarin, omeprazole,
esomeprazole, lansoprazole, voriconazole, flurbiprofen, glyburide, siponimod, celecoxib,
bupropion and its metabolite, desipramine, digoxin, dabigatran etexilate and dabigatran,
pravastatin, and rosuvastatin were used without modification unless otherwise noted. The
published models of valproic acid (UGT1A4 inhibitor) and ribociclib were also used.
Simulations were performed using healthy subject population (sim-Healthy Volunteers) model or
the cancer population model published by Schwenger et al., in which CYP expressions of
CYP1A2, CYP3A4, and CYP2C19 were reduced by 20%, 30%, and 33%, respectively,

compared to those of healthy subjects (PMID: 29063606). Simcyp default Cirrhosis Child-Pugh
A, B, and C populations were used to predict the impact of hepatic impairment on steady-state
camizestrant exposure. Compared to healthy subjects, the Simcyp Cirrhosis Child-Pugh A, B,
and C population models show that CYP3A4 abundance is reduced by 22%, 49% and 69% in the
liver, respectively, and by 0%, 39%, and 52%, respectively, in the intestine. UGT1A4 abundance
is reduced by 41%, 41% and 82%, respectively, in the liver. UGT1A4 abundance in the intestine
is the same across population models.
Additional Comments:
•

CYP3A inducers rifampin, carbamazepine, phenobarbital, and efavirenz are known
to induce UGT1A4 in vitro and in vivo. The Applicant updated the models of these
inducers with UGT1A4 induction parameters and then verified these
parameters against clinical DDIs with UGT1A4 substrates. The results are summarized
in Table 19.7.

•

A ribociclib PBPK model built in Simcyp V18 and published by Samant et
al (2020) (PMID: 32557601) was used to simulate clinical scenarios
when camizestrant is coadminstered with ribociclib. To verify the capability of
the ribociclib model to predict CYP3A DDI of ribociclib as an object and a precipitant,
the Applicant was requested to perform the following simulations and compare the
simulated and observed results: (1) plasma concentration-time profiles and PK
parameters of ribociclib following oral administration of ribociclib 400 mg or 600 mg
QD; (2) the effects of ritonavir 100 mg BID on ribociclib PK; (3) the effects
of ribociclib on midazolam PK following 400 mg or 600 mg QD; and (4) effects of mild,
moderate, and severe HI on ribociclib PK. In response to the IR, the Applicant updated
the model to the one published in the FDA review for NDA209935/S-027 and verified
the model in V22 as requested in items (1) –(3). The model was considered adequate for
predicting CYP3A DDI of ribociclib as an object and a precipitant. For a
detailed assessment of requested items (1) –(3), see NDA209935/S-027 PBPK Review.
For requested item (4), see the Results section for details.

•

A valproic acid model published by Huang et al (2023) (PMID:37735924) was adapted
with a fumic value of 0.12 and a UGT1A4 Ki value of 3 mM optimized by Conner,
Reed, and Zhang et al (PMID: 30460522). The Applicant was requested to verify the
performance of the PBPK model in Simcyp V24 by: (1) comparing the simulated and
observed plasma concentration-time profiles and PK parameters of valproic acid
following oral administration of valproic acid 1,000 mg BID; and (2) comparing the
simulated and observed effects of valproic acid on lamotrigine at various dosing regimens

19.4.4.4.

Results

19.4.4.4.1.
Can the PBPK model adequately describe the PK profiles of
camizestrant?
Yes. Clinical PK data that had not been used in model development were used to verify
the camizestrant PBPK model. The model could reasonably describe the plasma concentrationtime profiles of camizestrant following single and multiple oral doses in healthy subjects (Figure
19 and Figure 20). The model-estimated AUC values were mostly within 0.8- to 1.25-fold of the
observed values (Table 42).
Additional Comments:
• Camizestrant PK was initially simulated using the cancer patient population model
published by Schwenger et al. (PMID: 29063606). In this cancer population model,
CYP3A abundance is 30% lower than that in the Simcyp models for healthy subjects
and cancer patients. However, clinical studies showed that, at 75 mg across SERENA1 parts A+B, Japan Phase 1, and China Phase 1, the CL/F ranged from 70.56 L/h to
102.9 L/h (see Table 8), which was similar to that observed in healthy volunteers (88.8
L/h to 118 L/h) and to the popPK value (69.1 L/h). In response to FDA’s IR, the
Applicant compared the simulated camizestrant PK in cancer patients using the cancer
population model without reduction in CYP expressions (Simcyp default cancer
population model). The results are shown in Table 42. Although the model-estimated
AUCs in cancer patients were within 0.8- to 1.25-fold of the observed values, the
values predicted using the model with 30% reduction in CYP3A4 abundance tended to
be higher than both the observed values and those predicted using the Simcyp default
cancer population model.

Additional Comments:
•

To accurately predict the effects of CYP3A4 inhibitors and inducers on camizestrant PK,
the fraction metabolized by CYP3A4 (fm,CYP3A) is one of the key parameters that must be
verified in the camizestrant PBPK model. The fm,CYP3A value in the camizestrant model
was estimated to be approximately 51% based on in vitro recombinant CYP phenotyping
data and kinetic data from human liver microsomes (HLM). The camizestrant model with
a mean fm,CYP3A value of approximately 52% could reproduce the inhibitory effects of
itraconazole on camizestrant PK in healthy subjects (Table 43), which could reproduce
the inhibitory effects of ribociclib on camizestrant PK in cancer patients (discussed
below), thereby verifying the fm,CYP3A value.

•

Camizestrant is also a substrate of UGT1A4. The fm,UGT1A4 value in
the camizestrant model is 0.22, which is similar to the fm,UGT1A4 value derived from the
human ADME study. Approximately 14.5% of the administered dose was excreted in
human feces as unchanged camizestrant, which was considered to have originated from
bacterial breakdown. One supporting reason is that fecal stability analysis showed that
the glucuronide metabolite M4 of camizestrant was unstable in feces and was converted
to the parent compound over time, indicating the possibility of formation of unchanged
parent compound from bacterial breakdown in the intestine. Approximately 2.3% of the
dose was excreted in human urine as M4. The fraction of camizestrant metabolized by the
glucuronidation pathway is approximately 20% of the absorbed dose. The ability of
the camizestrant model to reproduce clinical observations confirmed the fm,UGT1A4 value:
in response to the FDA information request, the Applicant showed that the predicted
effects of carbamazepine, an inducer of both CYP3A4 and UGT1A4,
on camizestrant exposure were similar to those observed in the carbamazepine study
(Table 44).

Reference ID: 5864804

2.6-fold with ribociclib 400 mg QD or by 3.2-fold with ribociclib 600 mg QD
when camizestrant 75 mg QD was coadministered. In contrast, popPK analysis showed
that ribociclib increases Cmax and AUC [geometric mean (90% confidence interval)]
of camizestrant 75 mg QD by approximately 2.17-fold (1.03, 3.89) and 2.06-fold (1.15, 4.14)
(D8534C00001), respectively, but the dose effect was not analyzed, likely due to small sample
size.
Ribociclib was reported to increase the exposure of the CYP3A substrate midazolam by 3.8-fold
at 400 mg ribociclib once daily and by 5.9-fold at 600 mg once daily (PMID 32557601),
indicating that ribociclib is a less potent CYP3A inhibitor compared to itraconazole and
ketoconazole (Table 48). However, the effects of ribociclib on camizestrant exposure estimated
using the NCA method were greater than those of itraconazole. To investigate this discrepancy,
the Applicant was requested to simulate and compare the effects of steady-state ketoconazole,
itraconazole, and ribociclib on midazolam and camizestrant. As shown in Table 48, the PBPK
models of ketoconazole, itraconazole, and ribociclib could reasonably predict the effects of these
drugs on midazolam. Despite the large differences in their effects on midazolam, all three drugs
were predicted to have similar effects (~2-fold) on camizestrant, which is consistent with
the fm,CYP3A of camizestrant being less than 50%. This suggests that the apparent dose-dependent
effects of ribociclib on camizestrant observed in the NCA analysis are likely an artifact of
comparing camizestrant exposure across patients from different treatment arms. The results from
the popPK analysis likely represent the true effects of ribociclib on camizestrant PK.
In conclusion, when coadministered with ribociclib and a moderate CYP3A
inducer, camizestrant exposure is not expected to be reduced, and no dose adjustment is needed.

•

The verification of the desipramine model and the assessment of CYP2D6-related drug
interactions have been discussed in the PBPK review of capivasertib (NDA218197 PBPK
review). That review found that large variability in CYP2D6 Ki was mainly due to
nonspecific binding to human liver microsome proteins, and the in vitro Ki,u values were
approximately 2- to 40-fold greater than those used in the PBPK models of CYP2D6
inhibitors. Because in vitro CYP2D6 Ki,u value of camizestrant was obtained under the
experimental condition similar to that used for CYP3A4 Ki,u, the 8-fold difference
between the in vitro CYP3A4 Ki,u and the model optimized CYP3A4 Ki was applied to
the CYP2D6 inhibition assessment. Camizestrant was predicted to have a minimal effect
on the exposure of desipramine when camizestrant 75 mg once daily was coadministered
with desipramine (Table 19.).

Potential interactions between camizestrant and substrates of P-gp and BCRP cannot be ruled
out. The details are discussed below.

Effects of camizestrant on OATP1B substrate pravastatin
The PBPK analysis cannot rule out a potential inhibitory effect of camizestrant on OATP1B
substrates for the reasons discussed below.
Drug interactions of pravastatin with OATP1B1/3 inhibitors were frequently not predicted using
the pravastatin model in the Simcyp library, even when the lowest in vitro transporter inhibition
parameters of the inhibitors in the Certara DDI Database were applied in the simulations. The
observed interactions with pravastatin could only be reproduced, within 0.8 – to 1.25-fold of

was requested to perform additional simulations. Following administration of camizestrant 75
mg QD, steady-state camizestrant was predicted to increase pravastatin AUC and Cmax
approximately 50% when Ki values for both OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 were reduced by 10-fold
(Table 51).
Effects of camizestrant on OATP1B and BCRP substrate rosuvastatin
A potential interaction between camizestrant and the OATP1B/BCRP substrate rosuvastatin
cannot be ruled out for the reasons discussed below.
The Applicant evaluated the effect of camizestrant on the exposure of OATP1B/BCRP substrates
by simulating the rosuvastatin interaction using the rosuvastatin model in Simcyp V24, which
could reasonably predict the effects of different OATP1B1 and BCRP genotypes on rosuvastatin
AUC (Simcyp rosuvastatin summary report). However, the in vitro to in vivo extrapolation of
BCRP inhibition parameters (Ki or IC50) has not been established. Through the reviewer’s
independent analysis of OATP1B and/or BCRP inhibitors with clinical interaction data for both
the OATP1B1 endogenous biomarker CP-I and rosuvastatin, the model-estimated in vivo
Ki values were found to be approximately 1- to 368-fold lower for BCRP compared to the
corresponding lowest IC50 values of these inhibitors in the Certara DDI Database (data not
shown). The reasons for this discrepancy are unknown. When drugs had less than 10-fold
differences between in vitro to in vivo Ki parameters, their in vitro BCRP IC50 values were
generated considering at least one of the following conditions: using membrane vesicles or
accounting for nonspecific binding by either incorporating a pre-incubation step or directly
measuring nonspecific binding in the experimental system.
The BCRP IC50 value of camizestrant was generated using the Caco-2 monolayer system with
rosuvastatin as a probe substrate without a pre-incubation step or accounting for nonspecific

binding in the experimental system. Camizestrant had a high permeability in the Caco-2
monolayer system, therefore P-gp in Caco-2 cells may not limit the access of camizestrant into
the cells. However, because the reasons for the discrepancy between in vitro and in vivo
inhibition parameters are unknown, for risk assessment, the Applicant was requested to simulate
the effects of camizestrant on rosuvastatin exposure when the BCRP Ki was 20-, 50-, 100-, and
200-fold lower than the in vitro IC50. As shown in Table 51, a potentially significant interaction
between camizestrant and rosuvastatin cannot be ruled out.
Effects of camizestrant on P-gp
A significant interaction between camizestrant and the P-gp substrate dabigatran etexilate cannot
be ruled out for the reasons discussed below.
The ability of the PBPK models of dabigatran etexilate and dabigatran to predict P-gp
interactions was assessed using clinical DDIs of dabigatran with verapamil, clarithromycin,
quinidine, and ketoconazole (Dabigatran Simcyp summary report). Large range of in-vitro IC50
values of these inhibitors is reported in the Certara DDI Database. The difference in IC50
between the minimum and maximum values of each inhibitor ranges from 10- to 7,450-fold if all
the results are considered, and 3- to 93-fold if only experiments conducted under conditions
similar to those of camizestrant (e.g. P-gp expressing MDCKII cell monolayer with digoxin as a
probe substrate; study report BS001884-88) are considered. Compared with the Ki values used in
the models of the aforementioned inhibitors, the minimum IC50 values were approximately 2 to
133-fold higher with a broader difference observed when considering the maximum IC50 values.
In addition, the reviewer simulated the interaction effects of rifampin and belumosudil on
dabigatran (PMID: 32705692 and NDA214783). Given the large variability in P-gp IC50, the
predictability of clinical DDI from in vitro data is of concern. For risk assessment, the Applicant
was requested to simulate the effects of camizestrant on dabigatran exposure when the P-gp Ki
was reduced by up to 200-fold relative to the in vitro IC50. As shown in Table 19.19, a
potentially significant interaction between camizestrant and the P-gp substrate dabigatran
etexilate cannot be ruled out.

19.4.4.4.5.
Can PBPK analyses predict the effects of hepatic impairment,
with or without ribociclib co-administration, on the PK of
camizestrant and support dosage recommendations?
No dosage adjustment is necessary in patients with various degrees of hepatic impairment (mild,
moderate, or severe)when receiving camizestrant in combination with abemaciclib or
palbociclib. For patients with severe hepatic impairment receiving ribociclib concomitantly, we
recommend reducing the dosing frequency of camizestrant to 75 mg once every other day.
Additional Comments:
Camizestrant is primarily metabolized by CYP3A4 and UGT1A4. In the PBPK model of
camizestrant, CYP3A4 accounts for approximately 50% of camizestrant elimination, UGT1A4
for 21%, additional HLM for 12%, and renal excretion for 8.4%. Liver cirrhosis impairs liver
function and reduces the activity or abundance of metabolic enzymes. In the Simcyp Cirrhosis
CP population models, enzyme abundance is reduced to 0.78, 0.51, and 0.31 for CYP3A4, and to
0.59, 0.59, and 0.18 for UGT1A4 in CP-A, CP-B, and CP-C, respectively, relative to the Simcyp
Northeastern Caucasian population (Simcyp V24 Cirrhosis Population summary).
The predictive performance of the Simcyp Cirrhosis models has not been thoroughly evaluated
by the software provider. To assess the performance of these models for CYP3A4 substrates, the
reviewer conducted an independent analysis of twelve drugs that are primarily metabolized by
CYP3A (fm,CYP3A > 0.7) and have PK data in patients with hepatic impairment. It was found
that these models generally tended to overpredict the effects in patients with moderate (CP-B)
and severe (CP-C) hepatic impairment. The predicted drug exposures were greater than 1.5-fold
of the observed values in 69% cases for CP-B and 67% cases for CP-C, and greater than 2-fold
of the observed values in 46% cases for CP-B and 33% cases for CP-C (data not shown). To
assess the performance of the Simcyp Cirrhosis models for UGT1A4 substrates, the Applicant
was requested to compare the simulated and observed effects of hepatic impairment on the
exposure of UGT1A4 substrates: lamotrigine (PMID: 11421997), olanzapine (PMID:
31686778), and posaconazole (PMID: 19886860). The Applicant did not perform the analysis for
posaconazole because its PK is likely to be impacted by changes in bile salt secretion in hepatic
impairment due to its low solubility, a factor that is irrelevant for camizestrant given its high
solubility. The performance of the Simcyp Cirrhosis CP-A, CP-B, and CP-C models in
predicting the effects of hepatic impairment on the exposure of olanzapine and lamotrigine is
presented inTable 52. The effects of moderate and severe hepatic impairment on lamotrigine
exposure were overpredicted, with all predictions exceeding 1.5-fold of the observed values.

The FDA review was conducted in conjunction with other regulatory authorities under Project ORBIS. The FDA
collaborated with Australia’s Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA), Brazilian Health Regulatory Agency
(ANVISA), Health Canada (HC), Singapore’s Health Sciences Authority (HSA), and Switzerland’s Swissmedic
(SMC). While the conclusions and recommendations expressed herein reflect the FDA’s completed review of the
application, the applications may still be under review at the other regulatory agencies.

Reference ID: 5864804

Table 53 presents the Applicant’s predicted effects of hepatic impairment on camizestrant
exposure, both with and without coadministration of ribociclib. The reviewer identified a key
limitation in the Applicant’s camizestrant PBPK model that could lead to an underestimation of
these effects. Specifically, the Applicant’s model includes the uncharacterized clearance
pathways such as an additional human liver microsome (HLM) clearance and a biliary excretion
clearance, which represent approximately 13% and 3%, respectively, in patients with normal
liver function (Table 54). The contribution of these uncharacterized pathways increases with the
severity of hepatic impairment (Table 54), exceeding 50% of camizestrant elimination pathways
in patients with severe hepatic impairment receiving ribociclib. This could be problematic
because these pathways are assumed to be unaffected by hepatic impairment. Therefore, the
potential impact of hepatic impairment may be underestimated. This concern is supported by the
observation that the Applicant’s camizestrant model tended to underpredict the observed effects
of hepatic impairment on camizestrant exposure following administration of a single 75 mg dose
of camizestrant (Table 53).
To address this limitation, the reviewer developed a modified camizestrant PBPK model
(reviewer’s model). In this model, the uncharacterized pathways (16.5%) were attributed to a
CYP2A6-like enzyme, which is assumed to have the same enzyme abundance in the liver as
CYP2A6. In the virtual hepatic impairment populations, the abundance of CYP2A6 is decreased
by approximately 30% and 60% in patients with moderate and severe hepatic impairment,
respectively, relative to patients with normal liver function. The contributions of additional HLM
and biliary excretion were set to 0, and the contributions of other pathways remained the same as
the Applicant’s model. The reviewer’s model could reproduce the observed effects of
itraconazole on the plasma concentration-time profiles and PK parameters of camizestrant and
the effects of hepatic impairment on camizestrant AUC following a single dose of camizestrant
in patients with moderate and severe hepatic impairment (Table 55). Using this verified model,
the reviewer predicted that the effects of hepatic impairment on steady state camizestrant
exposure were similar to those observed following oral administration of a single 75 mg dose of
camizestrant (Table 55). For patients with severe hepatic impairment receiving the combination
of camizestrant and ribociclib (400 mg), the predicted camizestrant AUC would increase 6.2fold, which is beyond the 5.7-fold safety margin (see Section 6.3.2.3). However, if camizestrant
dosing frequency was reduced from once daily to once every other day, camizestrant AUC was
predicted to increase by an acceptable 3.6-fold. Therefore, for patients with severe hepatic
impairment receiving combination of camizestrant and ribociclib, a dose reduction for
camizestrant to 75 mg once every other day is recommended.
